Introduction
This report examines the science results for New Zealand Year 5 students from 
the Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) in 2014/151. 
Along with the reports on New Zealand’s results for science at Year 9, and for 
mathematics at Years 5 and 9, this report forms part of a series of publications 
about New Zealand’s participation in TIMSS 2014/15 (Caygill, Hanlar, & Singh, 
2016a,b; Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016). International ﬁ ndings for science for TIMSS 
2014/15 have been published by the IEA2 along with a separate international report 
for mathematics (Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016; Mullis, Martin, Foy, & Hooper, 
2016). 
This report examines New Zealand’s science achievement in relation to other 
countries that participated in the study as well as trends at the Year 5 level from 
1994 to 2014. An examination of the TIMSS assessment questions in relation to 
New Zealand’s science curriculum is presented, followed by analyses of achievement 
by sub-groupings (such as gender and ethnicity) and student background factors. 
Comprehensive coverage of background questions about teaching and learning, as 
well as the school context for learning, is also provided.
1 
Internationally this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 2015. As New Zealand conducted TIMSS towards the end of 2014, it is 
referred to as TIMSS 2014/15 throughout this report.
2 
The International Association for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement (IEA) is an independent, international cooperative 
of national research institutions and governmental research agencies. It conducts large-scale comparative studies of educational 
achievement and other aspects of education.

What is TIMSS?
The Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study (TIMSS) is a large-scale comparative study of 
mathematics and science achievement at the fourth and eighth grades (Years 5 and 9) around the world. 
As well as examining student achievement, it also monitors curricular implementation and aims to identify 
the most promising instructional practices from around the world.
Conducted on a regular four-year cycle, TIMSS has assessed mathematics and science in 1994/953, 1998/99, 
2002/03, 2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15, with planning underway internationally for 2018/19.
A consortium is responsible for managing the international activities required for the project. This consortium 
consists of: the International Study Center, Lynch School of Education at Boston College, (Massachusetts) 
United States; the IEA Secretariat in Amsterdam, the Netherlands; the IEA’s Data Processing Centre in 
Hamburg, Germany; Statistics Canada in Ottawa, Canada; and the Educational Testing Service (ETS) in 
Princeton, New Jersey in the United States. In New Zealand the Comparative Education Research Unit in 
the Ministry of Education is responsible for carrying out TIMSS.
What value does TIMSS offer?
Although TIMSS is useful for international benchmarking purposes, the real value of TIMSS lies in its ability 
to provide a rich picture of mathematics and science achievement within New Zealand over time.
TIMSS (along with other international assessment studies) can provide information about the performance 
of the New Zealand education system at the national level within a global context. The information from 
studies such as TIMSS is used in the development and review of policy frameworks, and also to inform and 
improve teaching practice. Developments arising out of previous cycles of TIMSS include resource materials 
for schools and teachers, along with teacher in-service training programmes.
What does TIMSS consist of?
TIMSS consists of assessments of students’ achievements in mathematics and science, along with 
questionnaires for students, their parents, teachers, and principals to gather background information. 
The background information provides a context within which the achievement can be examined.
The TIMSS assessments were developed cooperatively with representatives from participating countries. 
Questions were ﬁ eld-tested with a representative sample of students in participating countries and 
the results generated were used to select and reﬁ ne the questions for the ﬁ nal test. Questions for the 
background questionnaires underwent a similar process.
Assessment framework for science in TIMSS
As part of the TIMSS methodology, the international consortium develops an assessment framework which 
guides the development of the test items and the context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013).
The TIMSS assessments are organised around two dimensions: a content dimension specifying the domains 
or subject matter to be assessed within mathematics and science; and a cognitive dimension specifying the 
domains or thinking processes to be assessed. 
These domains diff er for middle primary and lower secondary students to reﬂ ect the science curriculum 
taught at each level. The three content dimensions in the framework for science at the middle primary level 
(Year 5 level in New Zealand) are:
3 
Note that this cycle of the study is called TIMSS 1995 internationally as most countries participated in 1995. However, southern 
hemisphere countries conducted the assessment towards the end of 1994 so in New Zealand reports the study is referred to as 
TIMSS 1994/95. Similarly for the subsequent cycles, the two years in which administrations occurred in participating countries 
are indicated.

 »
life science – how living things function and how they interact with other living things and with their 
environment; fundamental concepts in reproduction, heredity, and human health;
 »
physical science – understanding of physical states of matter, as well as common changes in the 
state and form of matter; know common forms and sources of energy and their practical uses, and 
understand basic concepts about light, sound, electricity, and magnetism; an understanding of forces as 
they relate to movements that can be observed e.g. the eff ect of gravity;
 »
Earth science – knowledge about the structure and physical characteristics of Earth’s surface, and use 
of its most important resources; describe some of Earth’s processes and history; understanding about 
Earth’s place in the solar system.
In order to answer questions in the TIMSS test correctly, as well as being familiar with the science content, 
students need to draw on a range of cognitive skills. This is acknowledged in the framework under the 
cognitive domain as:
 »
knowing – covers the facts, relationships, processes, concepts and equipment students need to know 
about;
 »
applying – focuses on the ability of students to apply knowledge of facts, relationships, processes, 
concepts, equipment and methods;
 »
reasoning – requires students to engage in reasoning to analyse data and other information, draw 
conclusions, and extend their understandings to new situations. 
Exhibit 1 below shows how the TIMSS framework matches to the New Zealand Curriculum and the content 
and cognitive demands that are expected at the middle primary level (Grade 4 or Year 5). Refer to Chapter 3 
for more information on the framework, especially around the content and cognitive domains.

Who participated?
In TIMSS 2014/15, approximately 312,000 students, 250,000 parents, 20,000 teachers, and 10,000 
schools from 47 countries (two of which tested students at a higher grade) and 7 economies (known 
as benchmarking participants) took part at the middle primary level in science4. A list of countries and 
benchmarking participants at the middle primary level can be found in Exhibit 2. Benchmarking participants 
will not be referred to in this report.
In this cycle of TIMSS, both Year 5 and Year 9 students from New Zealand participated. Schools in New 
Zealand were sampled so that there was no overlap between the samples. In New Zealand, there were 6,321 
students, 460 teachers and 3,424 parents from 167 schools assessed at the Year 5 level in October and 
November 2014.

How was TIMSS administered?
Each middle primary student was assessed in two timed sessions of 36 minutes, and answered a combination 
of mathematics and science questions. The assessment was a pencil-and-paper test containing both 
multiple-choice and constructed-response questions. Following this, students were given a questionnaire 
containing questions about themselves, their opinions about mathematics and science, their computer use, 
their attitudes to school and leisure activities. Principals, teachers, and parents were also given questionnaires 
in order to gain further information about the context in which mathematics teaching and learning take 
place. In New Zealand, the assessments and questionnaires were conducted in English5.
4 
Some countries only tested students who were much older than the target population. For example, middle primary students should be 
around 10 years old according to the design of TIMSS (in the fourth grade or the year level where the average age is closest to 10). 
However, in some countries these children have not covered enough of the material to achieve adequately on the TIMSS tests so the 
country has decided to test much older children. Throughout this report the countries that tested at a higher grade and the benchmarking 
participants are not discussed and do not appear in any totals or comparisons.
5 
In 2002/03, tests and questionnaires were also translated into te reo Māori, but in order to make comparisons between each of 
the cycles, these students were excluded from analyses presented in this report.

What procedures were used to ensure the quality of the 
data?
Members of the consortium ensured procedures were adhered to by all participating countries. TIMSS 
procedures are designed to ensure the reliability, validity, and comparability of the data through:
 »
careful planning and documentation;
 »
cooperation among participating countries;
 »
standardised procedures;
 »
attention to quality control throughout. 
Procedures included:
 »
veriﬁ cation of translations and layout of booklets and questionnaires;
 »
monitoring of sampling activities;
 »
international and national quality control observers during test administration;
 »
checking of data;
 »
detailed manuals covering procedures;
 »
rigorous training for all involved.
Further information
A lot of information is gathered during the TIMSS administration, and a number of techniques are applied 
when collecting and analysing the data. The Methods and procedures in TIMSS 2015 report (Martin, Mullis, & 
Hooper (Eds.), 2016) contains a detailed account of the assessment framework and instrument development, 
sampling, translation of materials, survey operations, quality assurance, creating the international databases, 
and scaling the achievement data. In addition, when the database is made available, it will be accompanied 
by a guide on how to conduct analysis. Brief details of the technical information are given in the deﬁ nitions 
and technical notes at the end of this report.
In order to provide a context in which the TIMSS results can be examined, TIMSS also publishes the TIMSS 
2015 encyclopedia: Education policy and curriculum in mathematics and science (Mullis, Martin, Goh & Cotter 
(Eds.), 2016). This encyclopaedia contains short reports from each country describing mathematics and 
science education policies and practices in that country.

Science achievement 
of New Zealand Year 5

CHAPTER 1:
 Key Findings
The average (mean) science achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 was the 
same as that of the students 20 years ago, but was signiﬁ cantly higher than 2010/11.
The gap between the high and low achievers (range) for Year 5 students in 2014/15 has not 
changed since 2010/11, but is not as wide as it was in 1994/95 and 1998/99.
New Zealand Year 5 students’ mean science achievement was signiﬁ cantly higher than 14 
countries, but lower than the mean score of 30 countries, including all the other predominantly 
English-speaking countries who participated.

The national strategic plan for science in society, A Nation of Curious Minds was 
launched in July 2014 in response to the Government’s wish to “encourage and 
enable better engagement with science and technology across all sectors of 
New Zealand society” (Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment, 2014). 
The plan focuses on three action areas which include enhancing the role of education 
in science, public engagement with science and technology, and the science sector 
engaging with the public. Enhancing the role of education speciﬁ cally focuses on 
the Science Skills in Education initiative; Teachers in Industry project; reviewing the 
positioning and content of digital technology within the New Zealand Curriculum/ 
Te Marautanga o Aotearoa; and increasing the science and technology content in 
initial teacher education (Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment, 2014).
In 2014 New Zealand participated in the sixth cycle of TIMSS, a large-scale 
assessment of the mathematics and science skills and knowledge of middle primary 
and lower secondary students. This sixth cycle of TIMSS occurred 20 years after the 
ﬁ rst TIMSS assessment, so we now have information about their achievement over 
this period. Forty-seven countries, including New Zealand, participated at the middle 
primary level in science in this cycle, conducted during 2014 and 2015. This chapter 
will examine the science achievement of New Zealand’s Year 5 students in relation to 
that of other participating countries and over time.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Science achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
New Zealand’s science achievement in TIMSS can be examined as a snapshot in time compared to other 
countries, but also over time. This ﬁ rst section begins with New Zealand’s achievement in the 2014/15 cycle 
of TIMSS. For reporting, the achievement of all participating Year 5 students has been put on a scale with a 
centre point of 500. This point of 500 was set using the student performance in the 1994/95 cycle of TIMSS, 
and achievement in subsequent cycles was put on the same scale. Achievement was then reported relative 
to this scale using the term scale score points (see Deﬁ nitions and technical notes for further details on the 
scales and the centre point).
The average (mean) science score for New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 was 506 scale score points. 
New Zealand’s score was signiﬁ cantly higher than the TIMSS scale centre point, but similar to Portugal (508) 
and Belgium (Flemish - 512), and higher than 14 countries. However, New Zealand’s mean score (506) is lower 
than the mean score of 30 countries, including all the other predominantly English-speaking countries who 
participated. Scotland, a predominantly English-speaking country with a similar score to New Zealand in 
earlier cycles, did not participate in this cycle.
The highest achieving countries, Singapore and the Republic of Korea, had average achievement among their 
middle primary students of over 580 scale score points. Of the countries that tested in English, Singapore 
had the highest mean score (590). The next highest mean scores among the countries testing in English 
were the United States (546), England (536) and Ireland (529).
It is also useful to look at the range of achievement – the gap between the high and low achievers. The lowest 
outer limit of the bars presented in Exhibit 1.1 is called the 5th percentile, the score at which only ﬁ ve percent 
of students achieved a lower score. The upper-most limit presented is the 95th percentile, the score at which 
only ﬁ ve percent of students achieved a higher score. The range of achievement from the 5th percentile (351) 
to the 95th percentile (633) for New Zealand Year 5 students was 282 scale score points. New Zealand’s 
range of achievement is wider than the high-performing countries (apart from Singapore, which shares the 
same range), and all the English speaking countries. 
The range from the 5th to the 95th percentile can move around quite a lot from cycle to cycle. Therefore it is 
useful to also look at the inter-quartile range, the diff erence between the 25th and 75th percentile, for a more 
stable version of the range. The inter-quartile range of New Zealand Year 5 students (115) is still higher than 
most of the high-performing and English speaking countries.

Exhibit 1.2 provides information to help put science achievement in context. It shows selected structural 
characteristics of education, including expenditure on education, to help the reader understand similarities 
and diff erences between New Zealand and other participating countries. Countries are presented in the same 
order as Exhibit 1.1 for ease of comparison. The GNI (purchasing power parity) is a measure of income for a 
country, converted to U.S. dollars, and adjusted to allow comparison of real levels of expenditure between 
countries (see http://databank.worldbank.org for details).
Many of the high-achieving countries had much higher income per capita than New Zealand, especially when 
purchasing power was taken into account. The exception was the Republic of Korea, whose GNI was a bit 
smaller than New Zealand, and a lot smaller than the other high-achieving countries. In terms of the countries 
that tested in English, all but Malta had higher income per capita than New Zealand.
Compared to other countries, New Zealand students had a relatively low number of hours of science 
teaching per year (43 c.f. the international average of 76 hours). Note that this ﬁ gure of 43 hours includes 
teachers that reported that they did not teach science in 2014 or found it too diffi  cult to estimate as science 
was integrated into other areas of the curriculum or taught as part of an enquiry-based learning. 
Nearly all of the countries that tested in English had higher numbers of hours teaching science per year than 
New Zealand (as shown in Table 1.1). In particular, students in the United States had more than double the 
number of hours of science teaching than those in New Zealand.
Trends in New Zealand’s achievement over 6 cycles of TIMSS
Mean science achievement steadily increased between 1994 and 2002 (see Exhibit 1.3). Between 2002 and 
2011, however, the science achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students decreased. Achievement in 2014 was 
signiﬁ cantly higher than in 2011. However, results are still signiﬁ cantly lower than 2002’s performance, and 
not statistically diff erent from 2006. The 2014 result appears to signal a turnaround and does not continue 
the downward trend observed between 2002 and 2011. The mean science achievement of Year 5 students in 
2014 is about the same as in the ﬁ rst cycle in 1994 (506 and 505 respectively).

achievement at the 5th, 25th, 75th, and 95th percentiles. As shown in Exhibit 1.4, the range of achievement 
has not changed since 2011, but is not as wide as it was in 1994 and 1998.

Trends on the science test questions from 2010/11 to 2014/15
At the end of each cycle of TIMSS, test questions are released into the public domain. At the beginning of 
the next cycle, new questions are developed to replace released questions. In addition, each cycle of TIMSS 
includes some questions from previous cycles to provide a trend measure over time. This section presents an 
analysis of the trend questions included in both TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15.
There were 95 questions common to both 2010/11 and 2014/15. Of these 95 questions, 21 questions had 
similar proportions of New Zealand Year 5 students correctly answering them across the two cycles (as 
shown in Exhibit 1.5). Half of the questions (50) showed an increase; that is they were correctly answered 
by more students in 2014/15 compared with 2010/11. In contrast, a quarter of the questions (24) showed a 
decrease; that is they were correctly answered by fewer students in 2014/15 compared with 2010/11.
In addition to the overall mean diff erence of 9 scale score points between 2010/11 and 2014/15 being 
signiﬁ cant, these item statistics demonstrate that there was a change in student achievement in science 
between 2010/11 and 2014/15.

International trends in science achievement at the middle 
primary level
There have now been ﬁ ve cycles of TIMSS internationally at the middle primary level; 1994/95, 2002/03, 
2006/07, 2010/11, and 2014/15 (although New Zealand repeated the 1994/95 study in 1998 with our Year 5 
students, this was not an international option). The design of TIMSS allows us to measure trends over time. 
Exhibit 1.6 presents changes in mean science achievement for those countries that have participated in 
selected cycles of TIMSS. Slovenia is the country with the largest increase in science achievement since the 
1994/95 cycle, although most of this change happened prior to the 2006/07 cycle. Singapore, Hong Kong 
SAR, and the Islamic Republic of Iran have also had large increases in science achievement since 1994/95.

In addition to those countries presented in the table, Portugal (56 scale score points) and Cyprus (31 scale 
score points) have also had large increases in science achievement since 1994/95. Morocco, though starting 
from a low of 264 scale score points, has had a large increase since 2010/11 (89 scale score points).
Relative rankings among countries
Many commentators on the international studies focus on New Zealand’s ranking relative to other 
countries. In order to inform this commentary, Exhibit 1.7 presents New Zealand’s relative ranking in science 
achievement compared with the other countries who have participated in TIMSS in 1994/95, 2002/03, 
and 2014/15. Of all the 48 countries that participated in TIMSS 2014/15 at the middle primary level, only 13 
have participated in all these three cycles. In addition, standard errors are presented so that the reader can 
calculate whether apparent diff erences are real. For example, the score of 505 in New Zealand (1994/95) 
is not signiﬁ cantly diff erent from the score of 504 in Norway (see section entitled Deﬁ nitions and technical 
notes for details of how signiﬁ cance can be calculated).
Exhibit 1.7 shows that the mean science achievement in New Zealand has been above the mean for the 13 
trend countries in each cycle, apart from the current cycle, where it falls below. In addition, the mean for all 13 
countries has increased, so the ranking of New Zealand among these 13 countries is at its lowest in 2014/15, 
compared to the previous cycles.

Classifying New Zealand 
Year 5 students as low 
to advanced performers 
CHAPTER 2:
 Key Findings
Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were classiﬁ ed as advanced performers (reached 
the advanced benchmark), while 12 percent of students were classiﬁ ed as below low 
performers who did not perform simple science tasks. 
Compared with other countries, New Zealand was around the middle when considering the 
proportion of advanced performers, but had a high proportion of lower performers. High 
performing countries had at least a ﬁ fth of their middle primary students classiﬁ ed as advanced 
performers, and four percent or fewer classiﬁ ed as below low performers.
The proportions of Year 5 students reaching each classiﬁ cation in 2014/15 was signiﬁ cantly 
lower than 2002/03 (the TIMSS cycle with the best results for New Zealand students). The 
proportion of Year 5 students classiﬁ ed as intermediate or high performers, has increased 
signiﬁ cantly between 2010/11 and 2014/15.

International science benchmarks
The international benchmarks are four points on the science scale: the advanced benchmark (625 scale score 
points), the high benchmark (550), the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low benchmark (400). The 
performance of students reaching each benchmark is described in relation to the types of questions they 
answered correctly. Exhibit 2.1 presents the 2014/15 descriptions of the international benchmarks of science 
achievement.
Exhibit 2.1: Descriptions of TIMSS 2014/15 international benchmarks of science achievement
Advanced international benchmark — 625
Students communicate understanding of life, physical, and Earth sciences and demonstrate some knowledge of the process of scientiﬁ c 
inquiry. Students demonstrate knowledge of characteristics and life processes of a variety of organisms, communicate understanding 
of relationships in ecosystems and interactions between organisms and their environment, and communicate and apply knowledge 
of factors related to human health. They communicate understanding of properties and states of matter and physical and chemical 
changes, apply some knowledge of forms of energy and energy transfer, and show some knowledge of forces and an understanding 
of their eff ect on motion. Students communicate understanding of Earth’s structure, physical characteristics, processes, and history 
and show knowledge of Earth’s revolution and rotation. Students demonstrate basic knowledge and skills related to scientiﬁ c inquiry, 
recognising how a simple experiment should be set up, interpreting the results of an investigation, reasoning and drawing conclusions 
from descriptions and diagrams, and evaluating and supporting an argument.
High international benchmark — 550
Students communicate and apply knowledge of the life, physical, and Earth sciences in everyday and abstract 
contexts. Students communicate knowledge of characteristics of plants, animals, and their life cycles, and apply knowledge of 
ecosystems and of humans’ and organisms’ interactions with their environment. Students communicate and apply knowledge of 
states and properties of matter, and of energy transfer in practical contexts, as well as showing some understanding of forces and 
motion. Students apply knowledge of Earth’s structure, physical characteristics, processes, and history and show basic understanding 
of the Earth-Moon-Sun system. Students compare, contrast, and make simple inferences using models, diagrams, and descriptions of 
investigations, and provide brief descriptive responses using science concepts, both in everyday and abstract contexts.
Intermediate international benchmark — 475
Students show basic knowledge and understanding of life, physical, and Earth sciences. Students demonstrate some 
knowledge of life processes of plants and humans, communicate and apply knowledge of the interaction of living things with their 
environments as well as impacts humans can have on their environment, and communicate knowledge of basic facts related to human 
health. They apply knowledge about some properties of matter and about some facts related to electricity and to energy transfer, and 
apply elementary knowledge of forces and motion. They show some understanding of Earth’s physical characteristics and demonstrate 
some basic knowledge of Earth in the solar system. Students interpret information in diagrams, apply factual knowledge to everyday 
situations, and provide simple explanations for biological and physical phenomena.
Low international benchmark — 400
Students show basic knowledge of life and physical sciences. Students demonstrate some basic knowledge of behavioural 
and physical characteristics of plants and animals as well as of the interaction of living things with their environments, and apply 
knowledge of some facts related to human health. Students show basic knowledge of states of matter and physical properties of matter. 
They interpret simple diagrams, complete simple tables, and provide short, fact-based written responses.
Source:  Exhibit 2.1, Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016.
In order to describe more fully what achievement on the science scale means, the 
TIMSS international researchers have developed benchmarks. These benchmarks 
link student performance on the TIMSS science scale to performance on science 
questions, and describe what students can typically do at set points on the 
science achievement scale. These benchmarks classify students by the ability they 
demonstrated in the TIMSS assessment. This chapter describes New Zealand Year 5 
students’ science achievement using these benchmarks, compared to other countries 
and over time.
20
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Exhibit 2.2 presents the benchmarks for all countries in two ways. The graphical part shows the proportions 
of students achieving at each of the benchmarks (i.e. above the high benchmark of 550 but not above 
the advanced benchmark of 625). The table part shows the proportions of students achieving at or above 
each of the benchmarks (i.e. at or above the high benchmark of 550, including those above the advanced 
benchmark). Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students reached the advanced benchmark, the point where 
students were deemed capable of communicating their understanding of life, physical, and Earth sciences 
and demonstrating some knowledge of the process of scientiﬁ c inquiry. In comparison, more than one-third 
of the students in Singapore (37%), and over one-quarter of the students in the Republic of Korea (29%) 
reached this advanced level of science ability. There were also fewer advanced middle primary scientists in 
New Zealand compared with the United States (16%), England (10%), and Australia (8%).
Twelve percent of Year 5 students in New Zealand could not perform many of the simplest tasks TIMSS 
seeks to measure (they correctly completed less than half of the low benchmark tasks). In comparison only 
four percent or fewer of students in the highest-performing countries performed below this low benchmark. 
There were also fewer really low performing middle primary students (those who did not reach the low 
benchmark) in England (3%), the United States (5%), and Australia (6%).
Included at the bottom of the exhibit is the international median percentage of students at each benchmark. 
Fewer New Zealand students reached all the benchmarks, compared with the international median.

Changes over time for New Zealand Year 5 students on science 
benchmarks
Since TIMSS began, there has been some concern about the proportion of New Zealand students who have 
low achievement in science. We can use the benchmarks to compare how this proportion has changed 
over time. Exhibit 2.3 presents those Year 5 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown in the 
graphical part) and those students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in the table 
part) in each cycle from 1994 to 2014.
The proportion of Year 5 students reaching each benchmark in 2014 was signiﬁ cantly lower than 2002. The 
proportion of students reaching the intermediate and high benchmarks has increased signiﬁ cantly between 
2011 and 2014. The proportion of students reaching the advanced benchmarks in 2014 remains unchanged 
from 2011.
The proportion of Year 5 students not reaching the low benchmark (12%), at its best in 2002 (8%), remains a 
concern. These students below the low benchmark did not demonstrate basic scientiﬁ c knowledge.

24
Strengths and weaknesses 
of New Zealand Year 5 
students within science 
CHAPTER 3:
 Key Findings
New Zealand Year 5 students consistently, since 2006/07, show greatest strength in Earth 
science and life science, compared to physical science.
The life science score signiﬁ cantly increased between 2010/11 and 2014/15.
New Zealand Year 5 students did signiﬁ cantly better at reasoning, compared with knowing and 
applying their knowledge.
There has been an improvement in the averages for both knowing and reasoning between 
2010/11 and 2014/15.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

To measure trends across time, some of the items from each cycle are kept secure 
and used in the next cycle. The science assessment in 2014/15 had items from the 
previous two cycles, as well as new items. In total there were 176 test questions 
at the middle primary level in science (Year 5 in New Zealand). As outlined in the 
introductory chapter, the international consortium developed the TIMSS 2014/15 
assessment framework, which guided the development of the test items and the 
context questionnaires (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). The assessment framework 
was matched to the curriculum of participating countries to reﬂ ect the curricular 
emphasis across countries, and is further explored in Chapter 4. This chapter focuses 
on the strengths and weaknesses of New Zealand Year 5 students in science across 
the two domains that make up the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment framework, the 
content and cognitive domains.
Defining the science content and cognitive domains
The TIMSS science assessment is designed to cover a wide range of the science teaching and learning 
students around the world are likely to undertake. Like the New Zealand Curriculum, the assessment 
framework is arranged by areas of science, and details the content the assessment will cover, termed the 
content domains. In addition to content knowledge, TIMSS also assesses students’ cognitive skills, outlining 
the thinking processes and behaviours as they engage with the science content (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013). 
The content domains for science at the middle primary level are life science, physical science and Earth 
science. The cognitive domains are knowing, applying, and reasoning.
The life science domain focuses on students’ understanding of the living world around them. It aims to assess 
their knowledge about how organisms function and how they interact with other organisms and with their 
environment, as well as fundamental concepts of reproduction, heredity, and human health. 
The physical science domain focuses on students’ knowledge of how the physical phenomena that they 
observe in their everyday lives can be explained through an understanding of chemistry and physics 
concepts. Physical science at the middle primary level includes an understanding of the properties of, and 
changes in, matter, along with concepts about energy and energy transfer, and forces and motion.
The Earth science dimension focuses on the students’ understanding about the planet on which they live and 
its place in the solar system. The middle primary students are expected to have some general knowledge 
about the structure and physical characteristics of Earth’s surface, the use of Earth’s most important 
resources, be able to describe some of Earth’s processes in terms of observable changes and understand 
the time frame over which such changes have occurred, and demonstrate some understanding about Earth’s 
place in the solar system based on observations of patterns of change on Earth and in the sky. 
There are three cognitive domains in TIMSS science. The ﬁ rst domain knowing assesses the student’s ability 
to recall, recognise, and describe facts, concepts, and procedures that are necessary for a solid foundation 
in science. The second domain, applying, focuses on using this knowledge to generate explanations and 
solve practical problems. The third domain, reasoning, includes using evidence and science understanding to 
analyse, synthesise, and generalise, often in unfamiliar situations and complex contexts.
In 2014/15, TIMSS science also assessed science practices. These practices include skills from daily life and 
school studies that students use in a systematic way to conduct scientiﬁ c inquiry, and that are fundamental 
to all science disciplines. The TIMSS 2014/15 assessment included ﬁ ve science practices: asking questions 
based on observations; generating evidence; working with data; answering the research question; and 
making an argument from evidence. However, as these practices cannot be assessed in isolation, the 
assessment is done in the context of one of the science content domains, and by drawing upon the range of 
thinking processes speciﬁ ed in the cognitive domains. (Mullis & Martin (Eds.), 2013).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5

The distribution of assessment questions varied across the content and cognitive domains. As Exhibit 
3.1 shows, there was more emphasis on life science questions, and the least emphasis on Earth science 
questions at the middle primary level. As TIMSS assesses a range of problem-solving situations in science, 
about 60 percent of questions required students to use applying and reasoning skills, with only 41 percent 
covering the knowing of the content. This distribution was consistent with the approach used for the 
New Zealand Curriculum.

Science performance in relation to the content and 
cognitive domains
Achievement scores were created for the content and cognitive domains to provide a value so that they 
are comparable with each other. In the early cycles of TIMSS (prior to 2006), a score of 500 on one domain 
was not directly comparable to 500 on another domain. So a new methodology was created and applied 
to questions from the 2006/07 assessment onwards to ensure that these domains could be compared 
with each other. Therefore, it is important to note that comparisons between domains should only be made 
between the 2006/07, 2010/11 and 2014/15 cycles, but not with earlier cycles (Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 
2013a). 
As Exhibit 3.2 shows, New Zealand Year 5 students performed relatively better on life and Earth science 
questions, with similar average scores of 511 and 506, compared to the lower score of 497 on physical 
science. On the cognitive domains, New Zealand Year 5 students did better at tasks that required them to 
use their reasoning, than those that required them to demonstrate their knowledge of science concepts, 
procedures, and facts (knowing) or apply their science knowledge (applying- see Exhibit 3.3). 
There was no consistent pattern among the high performing countries. In Japan and Chinese Taipei, physical 
science was a relative strength, while life science was the weakest among the three domains. Hong Kong SAR 
was relatively strong in Earth science compared to physical and life science. In the Russian Federation, there 
was no signiﬁ cant diff erence across the content domains. 
Japan had a similar pattern to New Zealand on the cognitive domains, with students achieving higher on the 
reasoning domain, compared to knowing and applying. Singapore and the Republic of Korea were equally 
strong at applying and reasoning compared to knowing, while Hong Kong SAR was stronger on knowing 
than applying and reasoning. For the rest of high achieving countries, there was no signiﬁ cant diff erence 
across the cognitive domain areas (see Exhibit 3.3).

Changes over time in content and cognitive domains
As Exhibit 3.4 shows, between 2006/07 and 2010/11, there was a signiﬁ cant decrease in the scores for life 
and Earth science. Performance on life science questions has signiﬁ cantly improved since 2010/11, back to 
where it was in 2006/07, while the performance on Earth science stayed the same as in 2010/11. Performance 
on the physical science domain hasn’t changed signiﬁ cantly since 2006/07.
New Zealand Year 5 students saw a signiﬁ cant increase in the mean scale scores for the knowing and 
reasoning cognitive domains in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. This brought the knowing domain back to the 
level it was in 2006/07, while the reasoning domain was signiﬁ cantly higher than the previous 2 cycles. 
The achievement for the applying domain hasn’t changed signiﬁ cantly since 2006/07.

Among the high-achieving Asian countries, Hong Kong SAR saw a signiﬁ cant increase in the mean scale 
scores across all content domains from the previous cycle in 2010/11, while Singapore had no signiﬁ cant 
change across all content or cognitive domains since 2010/11. Japan saw a signiﬁ cant increase in life and 
Earth science, and Chinese Taipei and the Republic of Korea had higher achievement in life science in 2014/15, 
while staying the same or decreasing on the other domains. The Republic of Korea achieved signiﬁ cantly 
lower on Earth science, dropping from 603 score points in 2010/11 to 591 in 2014/15. Among other high-
achieving countries, Slovenia, Lithuania and Kazakhstan had a signiﬁ cant increase across all content and 
cognitive domains since 2010/11. As observed with New Zealand, all other high achieving Asian countries, 
except Singapore, also experienced a signiﬁ cant increase for the knowing cognitive domain.

30
CHAPTER 4:
TIMSS and the New Zealand 
science curriculum
 Key Findings
Many New Zealand Year 5 students were working at level 2 of the curriculum by the end 
of the year, rather than level 3. Those students working at higher levels had higher science 
achievement.
When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for New Zealand Year 5 
students, there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when 
analysis reduced the test to contain only those appropriate to New Zealand Year 5 students, 
the average student got around half the items correct (51%).
New Zealand Year 5 students did best on life science questions, the area of the curriculum most 
likely to be covered by their teachers.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

TIMSS examines all aspects of curriculum: the intended curriculum; the implemented 
curriculum; and the attained curriculum. In New Zealand, the New Zealand 
Curriculum (NZC) lays out what is intended to be implemented and attained, and 
this guides teaching and learning. 
In the NZC, the alignment of curriculum levels with year levels is ﬂ exible. Teachers 
are expected to tailor lessons to meet students’ individual needs (the implemented 
curriculum). Students in the same year level may be working at diff erent curriculum 
levels, as appropriate to their abilities and pace of progression. 
As in previous cycles of TIMSS, teachers were asked to indicate which topics they had 
taught to their Year 5 students in the current school year (the implemented curriculum), 
and the curriculum level the majority of their Year 5 students were working at (the 
attained curriculum). This section will examine the implementation of the curriculum, 
as well as the match between the TIMSS test and the intended curriculum.
Science curriculum levels and the TIMSS content domains
According to the New Zealand Curriculum, by the end of their ﬁ fth year of schooling, students will be 
achieving at early level 3 in the science learning area of the curriculum (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
New Zealand teachers were asked at which level(s) of Science in the New Zealand Curriculum most of their 
Year 5 students were working for each of the strands: living world; material world; physical world; and planet 
Earth and beyond. Exhibit 4.1 shows that the proportion of students working at level 3 of the curriculum 
varied across strands, and that many classes were working across levels of the curriculum. Just over a ﬁ fth 
of students were in classes where the majority of students were working at level 3 of the curriculum on 
the living world strand (22%), with only 18 percent at level 3 in the planet Earth and beyond strand, and 16 
percent on the other two strands. More than half of the classes were working across levels 2 and 3. Results 
from the National Monitoring Study of Student Achievement (Educational Assessment Research Unit & 
New Zealand Council for Educational Research, 2013) also show many students working at lower levels of 
the curriculum than intended.
We can use the TIMSS content domains to examine attainment on the curriculum strands due to their 
similar science content. For example the physical science content domain for middle primary in TIMSS 
consists of understandings and skills related to three topic areas: classiﬁ cation and properties of matter 
and changes in matter; forms of energy and energy transfer; and forces and motion (see Jones, Wheeler, 
& Centurino, 2013). Similarly the physical world and material world strands at level 3 of the NZC consist 
of: physical inquiry and physics concepts, properties and changes of matter; and chemistry and society 
(Ministry of Education, 2007). 
Exhibit 4.1 shows that students whose classes were working at higher levels of the curriculum had higher 
achievement on the associated TIMSS content domain. Examining these results in the international context 
shows that if we had only included those Year 5 students working at level 3 of the curriculum in the 
TIMSS testing, New Zealand would still have had a lower mean score than the high-performing countries 
(Singapore, the Republic of Korea, Japan, the Russian Federation, and Chinese Taipei). For example, the 
average score for New Zealand students working at level 3 of the living world strand of the curriculum 
is still signiﬁ cantly lower on the corresponding life science content domain (539) than their Singaporean 
counterparts (607 scale score points).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

Curriculum match
Questions about international studies often focus on the appropriateness of the assessment questions 
for New Zealand students. New Zealand is not unique in asking this question; other countries are also 
concerned with the appropriateness of these tests. The TIMSS assessment questions are developed through 
a collaborative process that begins with the development of an assessment framework. The TIMSS 2015 
science framework (Jones, Wheeler, & Centurino, 2013) was designed to specify the important aspects of 
science that participating countries agreed should be the focus of an international assessment of science 
achievement. However, it is inevitable that the tests included questions that were unfamiliar to some students 
in some countries. In order to investigate the extent to which the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment was relevant to 
each country’s curriculum, TIMSS conducted a Test-Curriculum Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA was 
also used to investigate the impact of selecting only appropriate questions on a country’s performance.
For the TCMA, each assessment question was examined using the following two criteria:
 »
whether or not the topic of the question is in the intended curriculum for the majority (50% or more) of 
middle primary students in the grade or school level tested (in our case Year 5);
 »
whether or not the item topic was intended to be encountered by the students prior to the TIMSS 
testing (in our case October or November 2014).
All questions, regardless of this analysis, were included in any overall results reported for TIMSS. However, 
this analysis was used to ascertain the level to which the results might change for New Zealand if only 
questions judged appropriate for New Zealand students were included in the tests. The analysis also included 
an examination of how students in other countries would fare if they were only given the “New Zealand-
appropriate” test.
Exhibit 4.2 shows the proportion of questions considered appropriate to the New Zealand Curriculum in each 
of the TIMSS content areas. However, it should be noted that New Zealand’s science curriculum provides 
some challenges for deciding whether or not at least half of Year 5 students are likely to have met the 
question topics in the TIMSS test. The curriculum is not prescriptive, instead providing some broad guidelines 
of science concepts and skills that schools can choose to cover. Schools are encouraged to design science 
programmes that are relevant to their students and communities. Consequently, when schools plan their 
science programmes there is considerable variation between them. Another challenge is that the broad 
achievement objectives are grouped in levels that cover approximately two years of schooling. 
As shown in the previous section, New Zealand Year 5 students were generally working at levels 2 and 3 of 
the curriculum, so information from levels 1, 2, and 3 was used to guide judgements on the TCMA. In addition, 
curriculum-matched resources available on scienceonline.tki.org.nz were used for further clariﬁ cation (our 
thanks to the teachers who did this work).
Exhibit 4.2: Appropriateness of the TIMSS 2014/15 tests to the New Zealand Curriculum

Coverage of science topics
To ascertain the implemented curriculum, teachers of the selected students were asked what opportunities 
students had to learn the content in the TIMSS tests. Teachers provided information on science topics taught 
to Year 5 students prior to, or during, the year of the TIMSS assessment. For each of 23 topics, teachers 
were asked if the topic was mostly taught before this year, mostly taught this year, or not yet taught or 
just introduced. These 23 topics cover the TIMSS 2015 science framework (Jones, Wheeler, & Centurino, 
2013). They provide a link between the TIMSS framework and the TIMSS assessment. Note that this 
question is about whether the topic was taught, but does not gather information on the depth or breadth 
of the teaching on the topic. For example, the topic of how physical features and behaviours help living 
things survive in their environments might cover quite a variety of physical features and behaviours in one 
classroom, but only very simple ones in another classroom – this question does not gather that level of detail. 
Around six in every ten New Zealand Year 5 students had been taught all these topics in 2014 or the 
preceding years (62% of students). In comparison, just under two-thirds of middle primary students (65%), 
on average, across other countries had been taught all of these 23 topics (ranging from 33% in Poland to 
89% in the Slovak Republic – see Exhibit 9.3, Martin, Mullis, Foy, & Hooper, 2016).
More New Zealand Year 5 students had been taught life science topics in 2014 or the preceding years (72%) than 
Earth science topics (64%) or physical science topics (55%). Note that this question was not about the proportion 
of time spent on these, but rather whether the topic was covered or not. As mentioned earlier, life science is the 
area of TIMSS where New Zealand students showed the best performance, while the other two areas were lower.
The science topics covered by the fewest New Zealand Year 5 students were:
 »
understanding what fossils are and what they can tell us about past conditions on Earth (36%);
 »
electricity and simple circuits (44%);
 »
mixtures and how to separate a mixture into its components (44%);
 »
understanding that some characteristics are inherited and some are the result of the environment (45%);
 »
properties of magnets (46%).
Exhibit 4.4 shows a complete list of topics and coverage among New Zealand Year 5 students.
In the previous cycle of TIMSS in 2010/11, the topics of forming and separating mixtures (34%), light (37%), 
and fossils of animals and plants (34%) were among the least taught topics in New Zealand. None of these 
topics are worded the same way in 2014/15 as they were in 2010/11. 
34
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

New Zealand Year 5 students who were in classes where the topic was covered (the year of the assessment 
or in previous years) generally had higher achievement than those where the topic was not covered. 
However, Bulgaria, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Serbia, and the Slovak Republic all had 80 percent or more coverage 
of the topics, but were not among the highest performing countries. In contrast, the highest performing 
countries, Singapore (45%), the Republic of Korea (40%), and Japan (39%), all reported lower than 50% 
coverage of the topics.

CHAPTER 5:
Science achievement of 
Year 5 boys and girls
 Key Findings
Year 5 boys and girls had the same science achievement, on average, but boys had a wider 
range than girls.
Boys and girls in 2014 both had signiﬁ cantly higher science achievement than 20 years ago.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

New Zealand has many observed diff erences in achievement between boys and 
girls, with girls generally outperforming boys (Chamberlain, 2011; New Zealand 
Qualiﬁ cations Authority, 2011; New Zealand Qualiﬁ cations Authority, 2014). This 
pattern of girls out-performing boys has also been observed in other countries, 
with the exception of mathematics and science, where in many cases boys achieve 
just as well as girls (Bramley, Vidal Rodeiro, & Vitello, 2015). A Ministry of Education 
synthesis (Ministry of Education, 2007) highlighted several issues around the 
achievement of boys which included:
» early problems in reading; 
» lower achievement in reading and writing; 
» disengagement with school; 
» and lower qualiﬁ cation attainment.
The synthesis report was supported by a range of qualiﬁ cation and participation 
data showing diff erent proportions of boys and girls having success. Information 
from previous cycles of TIMSS has shown no diff erence in mean science achievement 
between Year 5 boys and girls. This chapter will examine the science achievement 
of Year 5 boys and girls in TIMSS in 2014/15, with some comparisons with previous 
cycles.
38
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Science achievement of boys and girls
Year 5 girls and boys had a similar score in science achievement (507 and 504 respectively). The range of 
achievement for boys was wider than for girls as shown in Exhibit 5.1. This pattern is consistent with previous 
cycles of TIMSS, where there was no signiﬁ cant diff erence in mean science achievement, but a wider range of 
achievement for boys than for girls.
Since 1994/95, the range of achievement has generally been decreasing for both girls and boys, although 
2002/03 remains the narrowest for girls and boys. As Exhibit 5.1 shows, between 2010/11 and 2014/15 the 
range increased for boys, but decreased for girls. For boys, the change was primarily at the top of the range, 
but for girls there was a change at both ends of the spectrum. 
Countries that were high-performing had no gender diff erence, with the exceptions of Hong Kong SAR, the 
Republic of Korea, and Chinese Taipei that all had a signiﬁ cant gender diff erence in favour of boys. Finland, a 
high-performing country, had the largest gender diff erence in favour of girls (12 scale score points).

Achievement on the content and cognitive domains for boys 
and girls
Although there were no overall diff erences in mean science achievement between boys and girls, girls

43
CHAPTER 6:
Science achievement, 
ethnicity, and language of 
the home of Year 5 students
 Key Findings
Nearly one-quarter of all Year 5 students identiﬁ ed themselves as belonging in more than one 
ethnic grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasiﬁ ka, Pākehā/European, Asian, and Other.
There is a wide variation in science achievement within ethnic groupings, with advanced 
achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings.
Māori had lower average science achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasiﬁ ka students 
had lower average science achievement than non-Pasiﬁ ka. There was evidence that this 
achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken into account.
Nearly one-third of Year 5 students regularly spoke a language other than English at home 
(most also spoke English).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

In the four year plan of the Ministry of Education, one of its stated priorities is “Māori 
and Pasiﬁ ka learning and success” (Ministry of Education, 2016). They state that 
“Equitable achievement for Māori, Pasiﬁ ka and students from low socio-economic 
backgrounds is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of 
Education, 2016). While Māori and Pasiﬁ ka are not a new focus, with previous 
documents and programmes aimed towards these students, it is important to review 
our progress towards this goal. Given that Māori and Pasiﬁ ka will be an increasingly 
large proportion of New Zealand’s working age population in the future, improving 
the system so that more of them will achieve will contribute to the future wellbeing 
and prosperity of New Zealand (Ministry of Education, 2016).
It is important to interpret this data carefully. It is not the ethnicity of a student per 
se that inﬂ uences outcomes. However, there is clear evidence that when teaching is 
culturally responsive, Māori students, and those of other minority ethnic groupings, 
thrive at school (Bishop et al., 2007). In contrast, Blank, Houkamau, & Kingi (2016) 
suggest that the unconscious bias of teachers lowers their expectations of what 
Māori can achieve. Thus the existence of a relationship between ethnicity and 
achievement, demonstrated in this section, does not imply that being classiﬁ ed in a 
particular ethnic grouping is a cause of poor or good achievement. However, a good 
facility in the language of instruction may be helpful in learning science. 
This section will examine science achievement among ethnic groupings and language 
users. Students were able to identify with more than one ethnic grouping, and so 
results should be read with this overlap in mind. Also note that only English-medium 
students are included in this cycle of TIMSS.
44
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Classifying students by ethnicity
Students were asked about their ethnicity by asking “are you...?” and then listing the major ethnic groupings 
in New Zealand. The list also included an opportunity for the student to record any Other grouping they 
might identify with. In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand classiﬁ ed each student to one grouping only, 
using prioritisation. In this cycle, any student who identiﬁ ed with more than one grouping was assigned 
to each of those groupings, in line with the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classiﬁ cation (Statistics New 
Zealand, 2005). Students were then assigned to the following groupings based on their responses: Māori, 
Pasiﬁ ka, Pākehā/European, Asian, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori and Samoan, they would be 
classiﬁ ed as both Māori and Pasiﬁ ka. Using this methodology, ethnic groupings cannot be compared with 
each other as 22 percent of students are in more than one grouping (some are in as many as four groupings). 
Exhibit 6.1 shows how students were classiﬁ ed. There were proportionately more students in each ethnic 
grouping using this methodology than if we had used prioritisation.
Exhibit 6.1: Proportion of Year 5 students classiﬁ ed in each ethnic grouping in TIMSS 2014/15
Ethnic grouping
Proportion of Year 5 students
Māori
23
Pasiﬁ ka
15
Pākehā/European
70
Asian
13
Other
4
Identiﬁ ed with 2, 3, or 4 groupings
22
The next sections examine the science achievement of students within each ethnic grouping, and 
comparisons are only made with those that are NOT in that ethnic grouping.
Science achievement among Māori
As shown in Exhibit 6.2, there was a range of achievement among Māori Year 5 students. The diff erence 
between high and low achieving Māori was almost the same as non-Māori. However, the whole distribution 
for Māori was lower than for non-Māori, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of Māori was much 
lower than non-Māori. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS and other studies.

As mentioned earlier, the TIMSS benchmarks provide an understanding of achievement beyond mere averages 
and ranges (see Chapter 2 for details of these benchmarks). There were 17 percent of Māori Year 5 students 
that were high achieving (at or above the high benchmark – see Exhibit 6.4). In comparison, more than one-
third of non-Māori Year 5 students achieved at or above the high benchmark. 
There were one ﬁ fth of Māori Year 5 students who did not demonstrate the ability to complete a reasonable 
number of the simplest science tasks that TIMSS seeks to measure (that is they did not reach the low 
benchmark). As shown in Exhibit 6.4, there were far fewer non-Māori who were very low achievers (9% below 
the low benchmark) compared with Māori.

Science achievement among Māori girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the science achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, Māori 
girls had the same science achievement as Māori boys. This ﬁ nding is the same as in 2010/11.
Science achievement among Māori students, taking into account socio-economic 
status
Some preliminary analyses were conducted to see if taking into account the socio-economic status of 
students aff ected the science achievement of Māori students. Fewer households are buying and storing lots 
of books as the popularity of on-line and electronic reading increases (see chapter 7). However, the number 
of books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement. These preliminary analyses included 
only books in the home and home possessions, but show that when these measures of socio-economic 
status are taken into account, the diff erence in achievement between Māori and non-Māori is smaller 
(33 scale score points instead of 47). This ﬁ nding suggests that ‘being Māori’ does not explain the diff erence 
in achievement between Māori and non-Māori. Further exploration is warranted to understand the diff erence 
in achievement between Māori and non-Māori. An understanding of the barriers to learning can help break 
those barriers down so that Māori students can achieve to their true potential. Further exploration should 
also keep in mind the idea that Māori students face impediments to learning due to deﬁ cit thinking by 
teachers (see for example Blank et al., 2016).
Science achievement among Pasifika
As shown in Exhibit 6.6, there was a range of achievement among Pasiﬁ ka Year 5 students. The diff erence 
between high and low achieving Pasiﬁ ka was a little smaller than non-Pasiﬁ ka. However, the whole 
distribution for Pasiﬁ ka was lower than for non-Pasiﬁ ka, and therefore the average (mean) achievement of 
Pasiﬁ ka was lower than non-Pasiﬁ ka. This is consistent with other cycles of TIMSS and other studies.

Science achievement among Other girls and boys
As shown in chapter 5, the science achievement of boys overall was the same as girls overall. Similarly, girls 
had the same science achievement as boys within the Other ethnic grouping. This is diff erent from 2010/11, 
when the average achievement of Other boys was signiﬁ cantly higher (37 scale score points higher) than 
Other girls.
Science achievement by language
Students were asked how often they spoke the language of instruction at home. In New Zealand, TIMSS 
was only tested in schools where English was the main medium of instruction. This question was formulated 
diff erently from previous cycles, so no comparisons can be made with these previous cycles. More than 
two-thirds of Year 5 students (69%) indicated that they always spoke English at home, and another 15 
percent almost always spoke English at home. Nearly all of the rest of the Year 5 students indicated that 
they sometimes spoke English and sometimes another language (14%). Less than two percent of students 
reported never speaking English at home. Those students who spoke another language, at least a little, most 
commonly spoke an Asian language or Māori every day at home. 
Those Year 5 students who sometimes or never spoke English at home most commonly spoke an Asian 
language or a Pasiﬁ ka language, as shown in Exhibit 6.22.

Many studies point to the advantages of bilingualism (or indeed multilingualism), including greater ﬂ exibility 
of thinking (see for example Adesope, Lavin, Thompson, & Ungerleider, 2010). The relationship between 
language used at home and average science achievement in the English-medium TIMSS test was complex. 
Because the science questions in TIMSS include many items that require students to ﬁ rst read the question 
and work out which science is required, a good facility with English is required. Therefore to fully understand 
the relationship between being bilingual, or multi-lingual, and science achievement, we need to know how 
well students understand each language.
References

of the cognitive correlates of bilingualism. Review of Educational Research, 80(2), 207-245.

Establishing a culturally responsive pedagogy of relations in mainstream secondary school classrooms.

56
CHAPTER 7:
Science achievement and 
socio-economic status of 
Year 5 students
 Key Findings
Children in homes with many resources for learning had higher science achievement, on 
average, than those whose homes had fewer resources.
Science achievement was higher, on average, for students in schools with more affl  uent 
students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The diff erence 
in science achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was higher than 
most other countries in the TIMSS study.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Possessions and books as measures of socio-economic 
status of students
Home possessions and books in the home can be used to give a measure of both the wealth of the home 
and the level of importance given to education and culture. The TIMSS questionnaires asked students about 
the presence in their home of books and a series of other resources which could be used to gauge SES. 
Arguably some of these resources could be considered facilitators of educational activities, such as an 
internet connection, but might not necessarily be used as such. This next section will discuss the results of 
these questions.
Books in the home
TIMSS has asked about the number of books in the home since 1994/95. In 2002/03, the question was 
changed slightly to include pictures of what each of the ﬁ ve categories might look like (see Exhibit 7.1). 
Exhibit 7.2 shows the proportions of students in three summarised categories of numbers of books in the 
home and their average (mean) science achievement.

The Ministry of Education’s four year plan from 2016 to 2020 states that “Equitable 
achievement for Māori, Pasiﬁ ka and students from low socio-economic backgrounds 
is a major priority which underpins much of our work” (Ministry of Education, 2016)
Results from the large-scale international studies, PIRLS, TIMSS, and PISA1, have all 
shown New Zealand has a large disparity in achievement between students with 
high socio-economic status (high SES) and those with low socio-economic status 
(low SES). Schools in New Zealand have received diff erential funding, based on the 
needs of the communities they are situated in, since TIMSS ﬁ rst began (in 1994), yet 
disparities persist. Therefore, it is important to continue to examine the relationship 
between socio-economic status and achievement of students. This chapter will 
present details of some measures used to examine socio-economic status, along 
with their association with science achievement.

Assessment, and has been conducted in 2000, 2003, 2006, 2009, 2012, & 2015 in New Zealand – see http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa.

Just under one-third of students (30%) reported that they had 25 or fewer books in their home. Just over 
one-third of students (36%) reported that they had more than 100 books in their home. This proportion 
of students with more than 100 books is the same as 2010/11, but much lower than in 1994/95 (62% - as 
mentioned earlier, there was the same wording in 1994/95, but no pictures with the question). It seems 
likely that fewer households are buying and storing lots of books as the popularity of on-line and electronic 
reading increases.
As shown in Exhibit 7.2, students that reported more books in the home had signiﬁ cantly higher science 
achievement, on average, than those with fewer books. This implies that despite fewer households buying 
and storing lots of books, the number of books in the home still has a strong relationship with achievement.

questions about how to answer this question from students who lived in more than one house as part of a

the international report for having one of the highest proportions of middle primary students coming from

than 70% of parents responding to the questionnaire; all other countries had higher response rates. Children

Socio-economic indicators of schools attended
Schools with larger numbers of students from low socio-economic communities are provided with extra 
funding per student. The decile indicator within New Zealand is used to allocate diff erentiated funding. Decile 
1 schools are the schools with the highest proportion of students from socio-economically disadvantaged 
communities, while decile 10 schools have the lowest proportion of students from these communities. 
Internationally, there was also information collected from principals that allowed examination of the socio-
economic status of the school intake. This section will examine these diff ering measures of socio-economic 
status: decile and composition by student economic background.
Decile
Previous cycles of TIMSS have shown that students attending schools with fewer students from lower socio-
economic backgrounds (having higher decile) had higher average science achievement than those attending 
schools with more students from lower socio-economic backgrounds (from lower decile schools – see 
Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013). As shown in Exhibit 7.6, this is also true of the latest cycle of TIMSS, with 
students from higher decile schools (9 and 10) having higher average science achievement (540 scale score 
points) than those from the low decile schools (1 and 2 – 432 scale score points).

School composition by student economic background
To make international comparisons across schools, there was also information collected from principals 
across the TIMSS countries that allowed examination of the socio-economic status of the combined student 
population in the schools. The School Questionnaire included two questions, one that asked about the 
approximate proportions of students in the school from economically disadvantaged homes, and one 
that asked about the approximate proportions from economically affl  uent homes. Principals were asked 
to choose from four categories: 0 to 10%; 11 to 25%; 26 to 50%; and more than 50%. The international 
researchers combined the responses from these two questions to give a measure of school composition by 
student economic background. The measure created was an index with three categories: schools with more 
affl  uent than disadvantaged students; schools with neither more affl  uent nor more disadvantaged students; 
and schools with more disadvantaged than affl  uent students. This measure allows us to compare the equity 
of our system with other countries.
As shown in Exhibit 7.8, nearly half of New Zealand Year 5 students (47%) were in schools with more affl  uent 
students, while just over one-quarter (27%) were in schools with more disadvantaged students. Average 
science achievement was higher for students in schools with more affl  uent students (534 scale score 
points) than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students (458 scale score points). 
The diff erence in science achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand (76 scale score 
points) was higher than nearly all other countries in the TIMSS study; only Morocco, the Slovak Republic, 
and the United Arab Emirates had a higher diff erence than New Zealand (100, 80 and 77 scale score points 
respectively). On average internationally, this diff erence was only 43 scale score points. Singapore (62), 
Australia (62), Sweden (64) and the United States (66), also had relatively large diff erences between the 
students in more affl  uent schools and those in more economically disadvantaged schools.

affl  uent students compared with schools with more disadvantaged students is consistent with TIMSS 2010/11, 
and with reading achievement in PIRLS 2010/11 (Progress in International Reading Literacy Study - see 
Chamberlain, 2013).

Schools with more affl  uent than disadvantaged students are deﬁ ned as those where the principal estimated that 25% or

As with the measure of decile, the TIMSS benchmarks show that there are high and low achievers within 
each of these three categories of economic composition of the school population. Exhibit 7.9 presents two 
diff erent ways of looking at this data – those Year 5 students achieving at each of the benchmarks (as shown 
in the graphical part) and those Year 5 students achieving at or above each of the benchmarks (as shown in 
the table part).
There were larger proportions of Year 5 students in the more affl  uent schools achieving at or above the high 
benchmarks (44%) compared with the economically disadvantaged schools (15%). As shown in Exhibit 7.9, 
there were Year 5 students in each of the three categories of school composition who did not demonstrate 
the ability to complete a reasonable number of the simplest science tasks which TIMSS seeks to measure 
(that is they did not reach the low benchmark). However, there were larger proportions of Year 5 students 
in the economically disadvantaged schools in this low achieving group (25%) compared with more affl  uent 
schools (5%).
Analysis of this school composition index, with other information collected from principals, shows that the 
schools with predominantly disadvantaged students face many challenges when providing education for 
these students. For example, these schools generally had a higher turnover of students (students leaving 
before the end of the year or transferring in part way through the year), and also were more likely to provide 
meals to students regularly.

65
CHAPTER 8:
Home climate for Year 5 
students learning science
 Key Findings
Students who often did early literacy and numeracy activities with their parents (according to 
parents) had higher science achievement, on average, than students who did so less often.
Positive parental attitudes towards mathematics and science were associated with higher 
science achievement. 
The average (mean) science achievement score was higher for the group of Year 5 students 
who read every day or almost every day, and declined as the amount of time spent on reading 
reduced.
New Zealand Year 5 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity, compared 
to other activities which were culturally focussed.
Tutoring in science was less common compared to mathematics internationally, and even less 
so in New Zealand. Only two percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had parents report that 
their child attended tutoring for science.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Informal learning activities in the home and parental engagement appear to have 
the strongest eff ect in the early years of a child’s development before attending 
school, and have been linked to student achievement (Lee & Bowen, 2006; Saracho & 
Spodek, 2010). Greater parental engagement in early literacy and numeracy activities 
and language acquisition has been found to have a positive impact on future student 
achievement in science (Council of Ministers of Education, Canada, 2013; Szechter & 
Carey, 2009; Tare, French, Frazier, Diamond, & Evans, 2011; Oludipe, 2009). Activities 
such as parents often reading books or telling stories to their children appear to 
have the strongest eff ect on achievement (Council of Ministers of Education, Canada, 
2013; Saracho & Spodek, 2010). Parental attitudes towards science have also been 
found to have a positive and signiﬁ cant relationship with science achievement 
(Perera, 2014; Chen, 2001). This chapter will focus on interactions with parents, 
parental attitudes to science, reading for enjoyment, leisure activities and tutoring 
outside of school, which may have an eff ect on student outcomes.
Activities at home prior to school entry 
In the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment, parents or caregivers of middle primary students were asked how often 
they engaged with the child in their pre-primary years on a variety of activities. Note that only 56 percent 
of New Zealand Year 5 parents returned the questionnaire, so values refer to only these students and their 
parents. There were 16 activities in total and they included: 
 »
reading books;
 »
playing word games; 
 »
telling stories;
 »
playing with number toys;
 »
reading aloud signs and labels; 
 »
playing games involving shapes;
 »
singing songs;
 »
talking about the things they did;
 »
saying counting rhymes or singing counting 
songs;
 »
talking about what they had read;
 »
playing with alphabet toys;
 »
writing letters or words;
 »
playing with building blocks or construction 
toys;
 »
counting diff erent things; 
 »
playing board or card games;
 »
writing numbers.
To summarise the responses to these 16 activities, the international researchers created the Early literacy and 
numeracy activities scale, based on reported engagement from parents or caregivers with these activities. To 
report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: often engaged; sometimes 
engaged; and never or almost never engaged.
On average across TIMSS countries, students whose parents reported often spending time with them had 
higher science achievement than students whose parents did so only sometimes or almost never. This is 
also true for New Zealand, where 61 percent of Year 5 students had parents who reported that they often 
engaged with their child in early literacy and numeracy activities. Thirty eight percent of Year 5 students had 
parents sometimes engaged with their child, and only one percent never or almost never did. 
66
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

As shown in Exhibit 8.1, high-performing countries like Singapore, Hong Kong SAR, Japan, and Chinese 
Taipei had a smaller proportion of middle primary students who were often engaged with these activities, 
compared to New Zealand. Therefore these high-performing countries had higher proportions of parents 
sometimes or never or almost never engaging the student with these activities. The students in higher 
performing countries whose parents never or almost never engaged with them still performed very well.
