Parental attitude to science 
In addition to gauging how often parents or caregivers engaged with the child on learning activities, it is 
useful to understand their attitudes towards science. In the TIMSS 2014/15 assessment, parents or caregivers 
responded to eight statements about their feelings towards mathematics and science, which were used to 
create scores for students on the Parental attitude toward mathematics and science scale. To report the scale 
in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: very positive attitude; positive attitude; and 
less than positive attitude.
As shown in Exhibit 8.2, parents or caregivers of 70 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were 
categorised as having a very positive attitude towards mathematics and science, which is higher than the 
international average. Only two percent had a less than positive attitude, while 28 percent of students had 
parents or caregivers categorised as having a positive attitude. 
Higher achievement scores in science were observed for New Zealand Year 5 students if parents or 
caregivers had a very positive attitude towards mathematics and science, compared with just having a 
positive attitude. As there were very few students with parents or caregivers with a less than positive attitude 
towards mathematics and science, it was not possible to estimate a mean science achievement score for this 
grouping within New Zealand.

A classiﬁ cation of very positive attitude corresponds to parents who agreed “a lot” to four of the eight statements, and

not ﬁ t the two criteria above.

students whose parents or caregivers reported a less than positive attitude towards mathematics and 
science (18%). They also had the smallest proportion of students whose parents or caregivers had a very 
positive attitude towards mathematics and science (14%).

Reading behaviour of Year 5 students and their parents
Parents were asked how often they spent reading for enjoyment each week. As shown in Exhibit 8.3, there 
was a consistent pattern across countries showing a higher proportion of parents or caregivers reading 
one to ﬁ ve hours each week than any other frequency. Also, increased hours spent by parents or caregivers 
reading each week was related to higher mean achievement scores in science for middle primary students. 
A small exemption is observed for Hong Kong SAR and Australia, where there was no diff erence in science 
achievement between reading for six to ten hours, and reading for more than ten hours. Compared to the 
high performing countries and the international average, a higher proportion of New Zealand Year 5 parents 
or caregivers reported reading more than ten hours per week.

New Zealand Year 5 students were also asked how often they read for enjoyment to gauge their reading

reading for enjoyment (see Exhibit 8.4). Students that read every day or almost every day had higher science

reading decreased.

Tutoring in science outside school 
Year 5 parents or caregivers were asked if their child attended extra lessons or tutoring outside school, and 
whether they attended to excel in class or to keep up in class. A smaller proportion of students in general 
attended extra lessons or tutoring in science compared to mathematics (see Exhibit 8.7, Caygill, Singh, & 
Hanlar, 2016). As Exhibit 8.7 shows, the top two high performing countries reported a higher proportion 
of students attending tutorials outside of school compared to New Zealand and the international average. 
These countries were also more likely to report that the reason for attending tutorials was to keep up rather 
than excel in class. Students from these countries were also likely to report higher achievement for students 
who attended tutorials to excel in class compared to students who did not attend. The proportions of 
New Zealand Year 5 students who attended tutorials for science are too small to compare mean 
achievement. Science achievement scores, on average, for most countries were higher for those who 
did not attend tutorials, compared to the groups that did.

74
CHAPTER 9:
Year 5 student attitudes 
to and engagement with 
science
 Key Findings
Nine in ten New Zealand Year 5 students liked learning science, which was similar to the 
international average.
Many more Year 5 students liked learning science than felt conﬁ dent in their science abilities – 
58 percent very much liked learning science, while 24 percent were very conﬁ dent in science.
Although three quarters of New Zealand Year 5 students were conﬁ dent in science, New 
Zealand had more not conﬁ dent, and less very conﬁ dent students in science, compared with 
international averages. 
Students had higher science achievement, on average, if they were conﬁ dent in their science 
ability, or liked science.
The science achievement of students had a stronger relationship with their conﬁ dence in 
science than how much they liked learning science.
There was no disproportionate representation of any ethnic grouping on how much the 
students liked learning science. However, the Māori and Pasiﬁ ka groupings stood out with a 
bigger proportion of not conﬁ dent students than very conﬁ dent students, while the Pākehā/
European grouping had a bigger proportion of very conﬁ dent students than not conﬁ dent 
students.
Similar proportions of New Zealand Year 5 boys and girls liked learning science and were 
conﬁ dent in science. This also held across both Māori and Pākehā/European girls and boys.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

In the current fast-changing, hi-tech economic and social environment, there 
is a growing demand for a more science and technology savvy work force. 
The New Zealand Government’s investment in the national science system has 
grown by over 70 percent between 2007/08 and 2015/16. The National Statement of 
Science Investment “believes excellent, high impact science is fundamental to 
our ability to achieve excellent economic, environmental, social, and cultural 
outcomes for New Zealand” (Ministry of Business Innovation and Employment, 
2015). It also envisions raising science investment’s contribution to the New Zealand 
economy, and becoming a world-leader in science and innovation. This increases 
emphasis on the role that early education practices can play in encouraging 
students throughout their school years to learn the science related subjects and 
pursue science majors as their future career. In this regard, understanding the 
inﬂ uence of diff erent factors on students' learning and achievement in science 
seems important, and may lead to shaping the practices that support conﬁ dent, 
connected, actively involved and lifelong learners, as the New Zealand Curriculum 
envisions (Ministry of Education, 2007). 
This chapter looks at students’ attitudes towards learning science and their 
achievement in the context of how much they like learning science, and how 
conﬁ dent they are in science subjects.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
75
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Student attitudes toward science
To gauge their enjoyment and conﬁ dence, students were asked how much they agreed with a series of 
seventeen statements about learning science, shown in the Exhibit 9.1. Ten of the statements were about how 
much students liked learning science and the rest were about how conﬁ dent they were in science. Students 
were given four response options: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot.
The New Zealand Year 5 students were generally positive about science. Nine out of ten students (91%) 
agreed (a lot and a little combined) that they like to do science experiments. Other statements with a high 
proportion of students agreeing (a lot or a little) were that they learn many interesting things in science, 
they liked science and enjoyed learning science, and that science teaches them how things in the world 
work. Similarly, the majority of Year 5 students also disagreed (a lot and a little combined) with the negative 
statements I wish I did not have to learn science (83%) and science is boring (82%). On the other end of the 
scale, for a quarter of students (25%), science was not one of their favourite subjects.
The proportions were not as high for the conﬁ dence in science statements as they were for the liking to learn 
science statements. Yet, at least eight in ten New Zealand Year 5 students agreed that they usually do well in 
science (82%). Three-quarters agreed that they learn things quickly in science, and 74 percent disagreed that 
they are just not good at science. Relatively high proportions of Year 5 students agreed with the negative 
statements science makes me confused (38% agreed), and science is harder for me than many of my 
classmates (36 % agreed). Similarly, a relatively high proportion of Year 5 students disagreed that my teacher 
tells me that I am good at science (36% disagreed).

Generally, Year 5 students with positive attitudes towards science had higher achievement than students with 
less positive attitudes. The science achievement of students had a stronger relationship with their conﬁ dence 
in science than how much they liked learning science. This relationship is further explored in the following 
sections. To examine students’ attitudes towards science, the international researchers combined the data 
into two scales: the Students like learning science (SLS) that included a set of nine statements, and the 
Students conﬁ dent in science (SCS) scales. For ease of interpretation, each scale was then divided into three 
categories, according to the degree of agreement with the statements. 
Students like learning science
The Students like learning science scale included the following nine statements:
 »
I enjoy learning science;
 »
I wish I did not have to learn science;
 »
science is boring;
 »
I learn many interesting things in science;
 »
I like science;
 »
I look forward to learning science in school;
 »
science teaches me how things in the world work;
 »
I like to do science experiments;
 »
science is one of my favourite subjects.
Students were scored according to their degree of agreement with the statements, and the results were then 
categorised into one of three groups: those who very much like learning science; those who like learning 
science; and those who do not like learning science. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 analysis used diff erent scale 
categories - like learning science, somewhat like learning science and do not like learning science (Martin et 
al., 2012, Exhibit 8.1).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Fifty eight percent of New Zealand Year 5 students very much liked learning science, and 10 percent did not 
like learning science (see Exhibit 9.3). New Zealand had similar proportions of students to the international 
average in all categories of the scale. Several countries like Portugal (82%) and Turkey (81%) had more than 
80 percent of their middle primary students very much liking science. The Republic of Korea, one of the high-
achieving countries, had similar proportions of middle primary students who very much liked (42%) and who 
liked (44%) learning science.
In New Zealand, and on average internationally, middle primary students who liked learning science more had 
higher achievement, on average, than those who liked it less. However, the pattern was not consistent across 
countries. Several countries like the Russian Federation, Serbia, the Slovak Republic, Sweden and the Czech 
Republic showed no signiﬁ cant diff erence in their student achievement, regardless of how much they liked 
learning science. 
Although the international researchers have changed the scale statements and categories since the previous 
cycle, they have calculated an equivalent average scale score that allows us to compare TIMSS 2010/11 and 
2014/15 results. The New Zealand Year 5 students’ attitudes have not changed signiﬁ cantly since 2010/11 
(Martin et al., 2016, Exhibit 8.1).

Students’ confidence in science
The Students conﬁ dent in science scale included the following seven statements:
• 
I usually do well in science;
• 
science is harder for me than for many of my classmates;
• 
I am just not good at science;
• 
I learn things quickly in science;
• 
my teacher tells me I am good at science;
• 
science is harder for me than any other subject;
• 
science makes me confused.
According to their responses to these statements, the students were categorised into one of three groups: 
very conﬁ dent; conﬁ dent; or not conﬁ dent in science. Note that the TIMSS 2010/11 study used diff erent scale 
categories – students who were conﬁ dent, somewhat conﬁ dent and not conﬁ dent in science (Martin et al., 
2012, Exhibit 8.4).
As shown in Exhibit 9.4, just over half of New Zealand Year 5 students (51%) were conﬁ dent in science, while 
one quarter (24%) were very conﬁ dent, and the other quarter (25%) were not conﬁ dent in science. New 
Zealand had a smaller proportion of very conﬁ dent, and a greater proportion of not conﬁ dent students, 
compared with the international average. Many of the high-performing Asian countries had relatively low 
proportions of very conﬁ dent, and high proportions of not conﬁ dent students.

In all TIMSS 2014/15 countries, middle primary students who were more conﬁ dent about their abilities to learn 
science had higher mean achievement than those who were less conﬁ dent. The score diff erence between 
those who were very conﬁ dent and those who were not conﬁ dent in science was similar in New Zealand 
and on average internationally (68 and 67 scale score points respectively). Compared to the Students like 
learning science scale, the Students conﬁ dent in science scale had a higher diff erence in achievement for 
students on the opposite ends of the scale. 
Overall, on the Students conﬁ dent in science (SCS) scale, New Zealand Year 5 students’ conﬁ dence in 
science in 2014/15 was very similar to that of 2010/11 (Martin et al., 2016, Exhibit 10.5).
Attitudes to science by gender
As shown in Exhibit 9.5, the proportion of New Zealand Year 5 girls and boys was similar on all three 
categories of the Students like learning science scale, which was also similar to the general New Zealand Year 
5 student proportions on this scale. 
The Year 5 boys who liked science more had higher achievement than those who liked is less. For girls, those 
who very much liked science had similar scores with those who liked it, while both had higher scores than 
those who did not like science.

Attitudes to science by ethnicity
When attitudes to science were considered, there was a consistent pattern on the Students like learning 
science scale among all ethnic groupings, which was similar to the general New Zealand Year 5 student 
proportions; more than half of all ethnic grouping students very much liked learning science, about one third 
liked it and around 10 percent did not like learning science (see Exhibit 9.7). 
Overall, there was a positive diff erence in average science achievement between those who very much liked 
learning science, compared with those who liked it less for all ethnic groupings. The biggest diff erence in 
achievement between those who very much liked and those who did not like learning science was among 
Pasiﬁ ka students (39 scale score points), while the smallest diff erence was among Māori students (20 scale 
score points).

CHAPTER 10:
Year 5 teachers of science
 Key Findings
About 60 percent of Year 5 students were taught by teachers with 10 years or more experience. 
The majority of teachers had only bachelor’s degree, but compared to 2010/11, more teachers 
completed postgraduate and higher degrees in 2014/15.
Only ﬁ fteen percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who had also specialised in 
science. Internationally, more students (38% on average) had teachers with a specialisation in 
science.
Overall, about half of New Zealand Year 5 teachers did not feel very well prepared to teach 
science topics. Overall, New Zealand teachers were slightly less prepared to teach physical 
science topics than life and Earth science topics. They felt less prepared to teach science topics 
than mathematics topics. However, compared to 2010/11, more Year 5 teachers felt very well 
prepared to teach science topics in 2014/15.
New Zealand Year 5 teachers were also less conﬁ dent to do science teaching activities than 
mathematics. Five out of 10 science teaching activities had less than half of the Year 5 teachers 
who reported having high or very high conﬁ dence to do these activities. New Zealand teachers 
expressed less conﬁ dence in science teaching than many of their peers internationally.
Compared to mathematics, far fewer New Zealand Year 5 teachers had professional 
development in science. About 60 percent of Year 5 students were taught by teachers who 
didn’t have any professional development in science teaching in the last 2 years. Compared 
with their international colleagues, on average, fewer New Zealand teachers engaged in 
professional development in science teaching.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

“To ensure every student achieves success our schooling system must provide 
high quality learning opportunities to meet the educational needs of all young 
New Zealanders”(Education Review Offi  ce, 2015). Within schools and kura, the 
quality of teaching has the biggest inﬂ uence on student achievement (Ministry 
of Education, 2016). The New Zealand Government established the Education 
Council of Aotearoa New Zealand in 2015 as a new mandated professional body 
for teachers to lift the status of the teaching profession, and publicly recognise the 
role of quality teaching and educational leadership in raising student achievement. 
Among many tasks, the council is also mandated to develop standards for teachers 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).
Along with student achievement, the TIMSS study also collects information about 
teacher education, professional development and experiences. This data may not 
provide enough insight into the connection between student achievement and the 
former, as schools may have diff erent strategies to make use of teaching practices 
and teachers’ expertise. However, it is useful to know if well-educated people 
are being attracted into teaching and whether they are being supported in their 
professional development, and if the schools are actively aiming to retain the highly 
experienced teachers. 
This chapter will report information collected about the characteristics of middle 
primary teachers, particularly focusing on their education, years of experience, 
preparedness and conﬁ dence to teach science, and professional development they 
have undertaken. Note that this section will talk about the science teachers of 
middle primary students who were usually, but not always, the classroom teacher of 
the students.
86
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Experience
More than one-quarter (27%) of New Zealand Year 5 students had science teachers who had 20 years or more 
teaching experience, and eighteen percent had teachers with less than 5 years teaching experience (Exhibit 
10.1). On average, the New Zealand Year 5 science teachers had had 14 years of teaching experience. This has 
not changed signiﬁ cantly from 2010/2011, when the average experience of science teachers was 13 years. 
On average internationally, more middle primary students (39%) had science teachers with 20 years or 
more teaching experience than New Zealand. New Zealand Year 5 science teachers had similar average 
experience as their colleagues in most English-speaking, as well as Asian high-achieving, countries. England 
and Singapore were the only English-speaking countries that had less experienced science teachers than 
New Zealand. Both countries had more middle primary students whose teachers had less than 5 years 
teaching experience (36% and 30% of students respectively, compared with 18% in New Zealand). 
Teachers’ years of experience appeared to have no strong relationship with the science achievement of 
middle primary students, both in New Zealand and internationally.

Education
As shown in Exhibit 10.2, more than one-quarter of New Zealand Year 5 students (26%) had teachers with 
a postgraduate university degree, and 58 percent had teachers with a bachelor’s degree or equivalent. 
Compared with the previous TIMSS cycle (19% of students in TIMSS 2010/11), more New Zealand Year 5 
students had teachers with postgraduate or higher degrees in 2014/15.
New Zealand had similar proportions of teachers in each category, compared to the international average. 
However, there were large variations across countries; a number of countries such as Poland (100%), the 
Slovak Republic (99%), the Czech Republic (92%) and Finland (90%) had a large proportion of teachers with 
postgraduate university degrees. On the contrary, there were quite a few countries including Hungary (98%), 
Belgium (Flemish – 98%), Japan (89%) and Portugal (89%) where the large majority of middle primary 
students had teachers with bachelor’s degree, but no postgraduate degree.

How well prepared do primary teachers feel?
To understand how well prepared the teachers felt to teach science, TIMSS asked about the 23 topics in the 
TIMSS Assessment Frameworks, as shown in Exhibit 10.4 (Hooper et al., 2013). The answer options were: not 
applicable; very well prepared; somewhat prepared; and not well prepared. 
New Zealand Year 5 teachers’ preparedness to teach the science topics varied across the topics. The topic 
that most teachers felt very well prepared to teach was the life cycles of common plants and animals (59% 
of students). The least common topic was understanding what fossils are and what they can tell us about 
past conditions on Earth, with only 31 percent of students having teachers who felt very well prepared to 
teach this topic. In general, the New Zealand Year 5 teachers felt less prepared to teach science topics than 
mathematics topics in TIMSS 2014/15. Overall, New Zealand teachers felt slightly less prepared to teach 
physical science topics than life and Earth science topics.

Some of the statements had diff erent wording in TIMSS 2010/11, therefore we cannot directly compare 
all statements. Comparing the statements that had similar or almost similar wording in both surveys, the 
proportions of Year 5 students whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach the topics appears to have 
increased for most topics in 2014/15. The increase was signiﬁ cant for ﬁ ve topics in the life and physical 
science ﬁ elds, as shown in Exhibit 10.4. Only one topic in Earth science, common features of the Earth's 
landscape and their relationship to human use, had fewer students in this cycle than in the previous cycle, 
whose teachers felt very well prepared to teach it (43% compared to 50% in TIMSS 2010/11).
In general, those Year 5 students whose teachers felt more prepared to teach the topics had higher 
achievement than those whose teachers felt less prepared. However, the relationship was not consistent 
across all topics and preparedness levels.
Confidence to teach science
Along with asking about preparedness to teach science content, TIMSS also asked teachers to express their 
level of conﬁ dence in doing each of the teaching activities shown in Exhibit 10.5. Response options were: very 
high; high; medium; and low. 
New Zealand Year 5 teachers were less conﬁ dent to teach science than mathematics. Five out of 10 activities 
listed in Exhibit 10.5 had less than half of the Year 5 teachers who reported having high or very high 
conﬁ dence to do these activities. The activities that most teachers felt less conﬁ dent doing were improving 
the understanding of struggling students, providing challenging tasks for the highest achieving students and 
assessing student comprehension of science, with about two thirds of teachers reporting low or medium 
conﬁ dence in doing these. These statements were asked diff erently in the TIMSS 2010/11 cycle, therefore it is 
not possible to draw comparisons between the two studies.

Internationally, New Zealand teachers expressed less conﬁ dence than many of their peers in other countries. 
Teachers’ feelings of conﬁ dence in doing the listed teaching activities varied a lot across countries, and 
across the type of activity. Japan and Northern Ireland had some of the lowest proportions of middle primary 
students with teachers who expressed having very high or high conﬁ dence in doing any of the listed activities.
There was no signiﬁ cant diff erence in the achievement of students whose teachers had very high and high 
conﬁ dence to teach science activities. However, students whose teachers had medium or low conﬁ dence 
achieved lower, on average, than those in other categories.
Professional development
TIMSS 2014/15 asked teachers about the types of professional development they had participated in in 
the past two years. The question included eight areas of professional development, listed in Exhibit 10.6. 
Compared to mathematics, far fewer New Zealand Year 5 teachers had had professional development in 
science in the last 2 years. About 60 percent of Year 5 students were taught by teachers who hadn’t had 
any professional development in science teaching in the last 2 years. The most common type of professional 
development in science teaching for New Zealand Year 5 teachers was around improving student’s critical 
thinking or problem solving skills (37% of students), while the least common type was on science assessment 
(11% of students). Compared to TIMSS 2010/11, in TIMSS 2014/15 more Year 5 students had teachers who did 
professional development in science content (26% compared to 16% in TIMSS 2010/11), science pedagogy/
instruction (27% compared to 14%) and science curriculum (24% compared to 16%). 
Overall, fewer New Zealand teachers engaged in professional development in science teaching than their 
international peers. On average internationally, more middle primary teachers had professional development 
on science curriculum, integrating information technology into science, science assessment and addressing 
individual student’s needs than in New Zealand. The diff erence between proportions in the other areas was 
not signiﬁ cant. 
In general, the teachers’ having professional development on science teaching didn’t appear to have a 
relationship with student achievement.

Initial analyses show that the amount of time teachers spent on professional development had no direct aff ect 
on students’ achievement. The Year 5 students’ achievement was very similar across all categories of time 
teachers spent on professional development. However, this may require further in-depth analysis, with multiple 
factors taken into account, to examine the true nature of the relationship.

94
CHAPTER 11:
Year 5 science teaching 
and learning
 Key Findings
New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in 
their science classes. However, fewer New Zealand Year 5 students reported experiencing very 
engaging teaching in science lessons than on average internationally.
According to their teachers, New Zealand Year 5 students spent just over an hour on science 
topics per week, which was almost half as much as the international average. Science was also 
more likely to be taught as an integrated subject in New Zealand. 
As well as spending less time teaching science than many other countries, New Zealand 
teachers used many of the science learning techniques less often, but had students look up 
ideas and information more often.
In contrast to mathematics lessons, New Zealand Year 5 students were more likely to work in 
mixed ability groups in their science lessons than work in same ability groups. 
There was an increase in the emphasis on science experiments or investigations for Year 5 
students in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11.
New Zealand had the highest proportion of Year 5 students with computers available for use 
during science lessons, compared to all other countries. 
On average internationally, many more teachers placed major emphasis on monitoring 
students’ progress than in New Zealand.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

The quality of teaching is a big focus for the Ministry of Education. Two of the 
four priorities for education for 2016 to 2020 are: “powering up learners, parents, 
communities and employers to inﬂ uence the quality and relevance of teaching and 
learning and lift achievement”; and “quality teaching, leadership and assessment” 
(Ministry of Education, 2016).
For eff ective teaching and learning to take place in science, research suggests a 
variety of teaching and learning activities are required (Çimer, 2007). Çimer suggests 
there are six main principles to eff ective science teaching: 
» dealing with students' existing ideas and conceptions;
» encouraging students to apply new concepts or skills into diff erent contexts;
» encouraging student participation in lessons;
» encouraging student inquiry; 
» encouraging co-operative learning among students;  
» off ering continuous assessment and providing feedback.
Çimer identiﬁ es a wide range of teaching methods and learning activities such as 
question-and-answer, discussions (either small group or whole class), small group 
activities, using ICT facilities, practical work, ﬁ eld trips, simulations, writing activities, 
role-play, inquiry-based teaching, and co-operative learning groups. The TIMSS 
study collects a vast array of contextual data to help analyse the factors that may 
contribute to students learning and achievement.
This chapter will report information about students’ engagement in their science 
lessons, teachers’ perspectives on instructional methods and learning techniques, 
the ways student progress is measured, and the use of school resources for science 
learning. Note that this section will present information about the science teachers 
of Year 5 students who were usually, but not always, the classroom teacher of the 
students. In some cases, classes were reorganised for science lessons.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
95
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Science teaching at the Year 5 level
According to their teachers, 43 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were taught science as a separate 
subject, as opposed to being integrated with other subjects. Internationally, 77 percent were taught science 
as a separate subject. 
Teachers were asked to estimate the number of minutes spent per week on science instruction, even if they 
taught it as an integrated subject. New Zealand Year 5 students spent an average of 66 minutes on science 
topics per week. The instructional time on science ranged from as little as 0 minutes, to just over 3 hours per 
week in New Zealand. The average international instructional time was 122 minutes per week.
New Zealand Year 5 teachers were also asked about the availability of assistance and experts in science 
teaching. Thirty eight percent of Year 5 students were in classes which undertook science projects with 
someone from outside their school to help the students learn speciﬁ c science concepts. Additionally, 34 
percent of students had someone from outside their school come and teach them a speciﬁ c science concept 
during their science classes. The questions didn’t mention the frequency of this happening. 
Students’ perspectives on teachers’ efforts to engage them 
in science lessons
Year 5 students were asked their agreement to ten statements, shown in Exhibit 11.1, to gauge their level of 
engagement with their science lessons. Their answer options were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; 
and disagree a lot. Overall, New Zealand Year 5 students were relatively positive in their responses about 
how engaged they were in their science lessons, with the majority of students agreeing (a lot and a little 
combined) to all statements.
There wasn’t much variation in student responses to aspects of science class engagement; 8 out of 10 
statements had similar proportions of students agreeing (a lot and a little combined) to them. The least 
agreed upon aspect of engagement was teachers letting students show what they had learnt (82%).
There was no consistent pattern in the achievement of students depending on their agreement to the 
statements. Overall, the Year 5 students who agreed a little to the statements had signiﬁ cantly higher scores, 
if not similar, to those in other categories.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in Exhibit 11.1 into the Students’ 
views on engaging teaching in science lessons scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values on 
the scale were grouped into three categories: very engaging teaching; engaging teaching; and less than 
engaging teaching. Fewer New Zealand Year 5 students reported experiencing very engaging teaching in 
science lessons than on average internationally (see Exhibit 11.2). The international average shows that the 
more engaged middle primary students were in their science lessons, the higher the achievement. However, 
the pattern diff ered widely across countries. New Zealand Year 5 students who reported being engaged 
(middle category) had signiﬁ cantly higher achievement than those who were less than engaged (15 scale 
score points diff erence) or those who were very engaged (10 scale score points diff erence). There was no 
signiﬁ cant diff erence in the mean achievement for students who were less than engaged and very engaged. 
For about a quarter of the participating countries, degrees of engaging teaching in science showed no 
signiﬁ cant diff erence in achievement.

Teachers’ perspectives on instructional techniques 
Teachers were asked to report how often they used certain instructional techniques. As Exhibit 11.3 shows, 86 
percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were in classes where teachers encouraged all students to express 
their ideas, every or almost every lesson. Other common activities that teachers did every or almost every 
lesson included encouraging classroom discussion among students (84%), asking students to explain their 
answers (79%), and linking new content to students’ prior knowledge (77%). The least common activity was 
bringing interesting materials to class, with only 15 percent of students having their teachers do this every or 
almost every lesson.

Teachers’ perspectives on learning activities
In addition to instructional techniques, teachers were also asked to report on the extent to which they used 
certain learning activities in their science lessons. They were asked to report how often they used the fourteen 
learning activities, shown in Exhibit 11.4, in their science lessons, with answer options: never; some lessons; 
about half the lessons; and every or almost every lesson. Some of these activities were also asked in TIMSS 
2010/11 cycle (see Exhibit 11.5). Because there were fewer than 10 percent of Year 5 students doing most of 
the activities every or almost every lesson, the results are combined for reporting the data on about half the 
lessons and every or almost every lessons into one category, called about half the lessons or more often.

In TIMSS 2014/15, New Zealand Year 5 students were more likely to work in mixed ability groups in their 
science lessons than work in same ability groups; 68 percent of students worked in mixed ability groups 
about half the lessons or more often, as opposed to 24 percent in same ability groups.
There has been an increase in the emphasis on science experiment or investigation for Year 5 students since 
TIMSS 2010/11 (see Exhibit 11.5). Fifty three percent of students conducted experiments or investigations 
about half the lessons or more often in 2014/15, compared to 39 percent in 2010/11. There was also an 
increase in designing and planning experiments or investigations, with 42 percent of Year 5 students doing 
this in 2014/15, compared to 34 percent in the previous cycle. The least common activity in this category was 
watching the teacher demonstrate an experiment or investigation, with only 25 percent of students doing it 
about half the lessons or more often. Out of all comparable activities, the biggest increase (a 20 percentage 
point) was noted in the activity that involved the students observing natural phenomena such as the weather 
or a plant growing and describing what they see, with 44 percent of Year 5 students doing it about half the 
lessons or more often, compared to 24 percent in 2010/11.

New Zealand teaching activities markedly diff ered from many other countries. It is important to consider 
that, compared to other countries, New Zealand middle primary students had a relatively low number of 
hours of science teaching per year, and there were a number of teachers that reported that they did not 
teach science at all during 2014/15 (there were other priorities for that year). In addition, some teachers 
found it too diffi  cult to estimate hours of teaching, as science was integrated into other areas of the 
curriculum, or taught as part of an enquiry-based learning. Hours of science teaching were also low in 
TIMSS 2010/11.
With this in mind, compared to their international peers, the New Zealand middle primary teachers less 
often used the TIMSS 2014/15 learning activities. They were less likely to have students listen to the teacher 
explain new science content, watch the teacher demonstrate an experiment or investigation, read their 
textbooks or other resource materials, memorise facts and principles and take a written test or quiz, half 
the lessons or more often in their science teaching. The activity that New Zealand teachers had a higher 
frequency of using in their science teaching was working in mixed ability groups (68% compared to 56% on 
average internationally).
The learning activities used by the teachers didn’t seem to have a strong relationship with student 
achievement, both in New Zealand and on average internationally.

Use of computers
New Zealand had the highest proportion of middle primary students (91%) whose teachers responded 
that computers were available for use during science lessons, compared to all other countries. On average 
internationally, 46 percent of middle primary students were in classes where computers were available for 
use during science lessons (see Exhibit 11.6). New Zealand had the highest use of computers to look up 
ideas and information, with 62 percent of Year 5 students using the computer for this purpose at least once 
or twice a week. Using the computers for activities like studying natural phenomena through simulations, 
doing scientiﬁ c procedures or experiments, and practicing skills and procedures was less common, with less 
than 20 percent of students using the computers for these purposes at least once or twice a week. This is a 
continued trend from TIMSS 2010/11, where these proportions were the highest in New Zealand compared to 
all participating countries.
In terms of the overall level of accessibility of computers at school, 86 percent of New Zealand Year 5 
students had teachers report that students had access to shared computers in the classroom. Sixty-eight 
percent of students had teachers report that the school had computers the class could use sometimes, and 
only 10 percent of students had access to their own computer at school. The students who had computers 
available to use during their science lessons had higher achievement than those who did not. However, it 
didn’t make signiﬁ cant diff erence to the achievement whether students shared the computers, or each 
student had a computer.

Monitoring student progress 
According to the National Administration Guidelines (NAGs), schools in New Zealand are required to gather 
information that is suffi  ciently comprehensive to enable the progress and achievement of students to be 
evaluated through a range of assessment practices (Ministry of Education, 2015). Teachers were asked how 
much emphasis they placed on diff erent forms of monitoring the students’ progress in science (see Exhibit 
11.7). The New Zealand Year 5 teachers placed more emphasis on assessment of students’ ongoing work 
than on tests, with 59% of students studying in classes where teachers placed major emphasis on this form 
of monitoring tool.
Internationally as well, the assessment of students’ ongoing work was the most common form of monitoring 
the student progress in science, with an average of 77 percent of students’ progress being monitored using 
this tool. On average internationally, more teachers in other countries placed major emphasis on all three 
forms of monitoring tools than in New Zealand. The emphasis on diff erent methods of monitoring students’ 
progress seemed to have no consistent relationship with student science achievement.

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
103
CHAPTER 12:
Year 5 students’ perceptions 
of climate for learning
 Key Findings
Nine out of ten New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about their schools, teachers and 
classmates, and two thirds had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a sense 
of belonging had higher science achievement, on average, than those with little sense of 
belonging at school.
There were more students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt 
safe at school, liked being in school, and felt like they belonged at their school.
New Zealand Year 5 students experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than 
most of the other participating countries.
Compared to 2010/11, fewer students experienced the individual bullying behaviours: I was 
made fun of or called names; something was stolen from me; and I was hit or hurt (e.g. shoving, 
hitting, kicking) by other student(s). For all other behaviours, the proportion did not change 
signiﬁ cantly between the two cycles.
More boys experienced bullying behaviours than girls. More students experienced bullying 
behaviours in schools with lower socio-economic status than those with higher socio-economic 
status.
Students who experienced bullying behaviours a few times a year or less often performed 
signiﬁ cantly better in science, on average, than those who experienced them once a month or 
more frequently. Bullying behaviours had the same impact on science achievement for Year 5 
students across diff erent gender, ethnic groupings and socio-economic backgrounds.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

A large body of international and local research indicates a close connection 
between school climate and academic achievement, as well as the behavioural and 
social-emotional wellbeing of students (Thapa et al., 2012; O’Malley et al., 2012; Ruus 
et al., 2007; Macneil et al., 2009; Voight et al., 2013; Marsh et al., 2014). Students’ 
perceptions of school climate appear to be positively associated with their academic 
motivation, sense of school belonging, attitudes towards learning and positive social 
behaviours and attitudes in general. Therefore, it is important to study the factors 
that inﬂ uence how students feel about their school learning environment.
TIMSS collects a vast amount of contextual data, including students’ responses to 
questions about their school. This chapter examines students’ perceptions of the 
school climate, in the context of their sense of school belonging and experience of 
bullying behaviours within the school environment.

www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Students’ sense of school belonging
To examine the relationship and connections students have towards their school, teachers and classmates, 
students in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to respond to a series of positive agree/disagree statements (see 
Exhibit 12.1). In addition, New Zealand students were also asked their agreement on the statement I think that 
students at this school care about each other. They had the choice of selecting either: agree a lot; agree a 
little; disagree a little; or disagree a lot for each statement. 
As Exhibit 12.1 shows, most New Zealand Year 5 students overall were positive about their school, teachers 
and classmates, with around nine out of every ten students agreeing with all these statements. Most Year 
5 students agreed that they liked seeing their classmates at school (97% of students) and that they learnt 
a lot at school (96% of students). However, 13 percent of Year 5 students disagreed about feeling that they 
belonged at their school, and 15 percent disagreed that students at their school care about each other.

To understand students’ sense of belonging better, the international researchers combined seven of the 
statements, as shown in Exhibit 12.1 (except the last statement in the ﬁ gure which was speciﬁ c to New 
Zealand), to create the student’s Sense of school belonging scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, 
values were grouped into three categories: little sense of belonging; sense of belonging; and high sense of 
belonging.

As shown in Exhibit 12.2, 67 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had a high sense of school belonging; 
29 percent had a sense of school belonging (but not high), and a smaller proportion of students had little 
sense of school belonging (4%). These proportions are very similar to the international average.
New Zealand Year 5 students with a high sense of belonging had similar science achievement, on average, 
to students who had a sense of belonging at school. Students with little sense of belonging at school 
had signiﬁ cantly lower achievement in science, on average, than students with more of a sense of 
belonging at school.

Students’ sense of school belonging by gender
As Exhibit 12.3 shows, more New Zealand Year 5 girls had a high sense of school belonging than the boys 
(74% compared to 61%), while more boys (6% compared to 2%) had little sense of belonging at school. 
The pattern followed that of New Zealand overall, with those with little sense of belonging having lower 
science achievement, on average, than those with more of a sense of belonging.

Students’ sense of school belonging by ethnicity
Reﬂ ective of all New Zealand Year 5 students, a large proportion of students had a high sense of school 
belonging across all ethnic groupings. As Exhibit 12.4 shows, Pasiﬁ ka Year 5 students had the largest 
proportion of students with a high sense of school belonging, compared to non-Pasiﬁ ka students (74% and 
66% respectively). Asian Year 5 students were similar, with 70 percent who felt a high sense of belonging, 
compared to 67 percent of non-Asian students. Pākehā/European Year 5 students, however, had a lower 
proportion of students who had a high sense of belonging to their school, compared to non-Pākehā/
European students (65% and 72% respectively). 
Students who had a high sense of belonging achieved similar science scores to those who had just a sense of 
belonging across all ethnic groupings. The number of students in each ethnic grouping who had little sense 
of belonging at school was too small to report and compare achievement.

Trends in students’ perceptions of school belonging – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
Statements on school belonging were also asked in the previous cycles of TIMSS, and proportionately 
more Year 5 students agreed with these statements in 2014/15 compared to 2010/11 (see Exhibit 12.5). The 
proportion of students who liked being at school in 2014/15 (though similar to that of 2002/03), is higher 
than 2006/2007 and 2010/2011. Students were asked whether they thought students cared about each other 
at their school, a statement ﬁ rst introduced in TIMSS 2006/07. There has been no signiﬁ cant change in the 
proportion of students who agreed with this statement over the three cycles of TIMSS since 2006/07.

Students’ experiences of bullying behaviours at school 
A sense of security at school promotes a stable learning environment. Bullying behaviour among students is 
a threat to the school environment, and causes distress to victims, leads to low self-esteem, decreased sense 
of belonging and poor achievements (Hooper et al., 2013). To ﬁ nd out the frequency of bullying behaviours 
experienced during the year, students were asked to respond to a series of statements (see Exhibit 12.6). 
They had the choice of selecting: never; a few times a year; once or twice a month; or at least once a week for 
each statement. 
Being made fun of or called names and being left out of games or activities by other students were the two 
most commonly reported bullying behaviours experienced at least once a month or more by New Zealand 
Year 5 students (38% and 37% respectively). The least commonly reported behaviours included sharing 
embarrassing information about me and being threatened, with one ﬁ fth of students experiencing these at 
least once a month or more frequently in that year.

For all of the statements in Exhibit 12.6, science achievement was signiﬁ cantly higher for all New Zealand 
Year 5 students who experienced bullying behaviours less frequently (ie. student responded a few times a 
year or never) compared to those who experienced the bullying behaviour at least once a month. There was 
a greater diff erence in mean science achievement for the least commonly experienced bullying behaviours, 
compared with the most commonly experienced behaviours (Exhibit 12.6).
The statements in Exhibit 12.6 were combined internationally to form the Students bullied at school scale, 
which describes the extent to which students experienced bullying behaviours at school. As Exhibit 12.7 
shows, New Zealand Year 5 students were more likely to experience bullying behaviours at school than middle 
primary students in the majority of TIMSS 2014/15 countries. On the Students bullied at school scale, 60 
percent of New Zealand Year 5 students experienced bullying behaviours at school at least monthly or more 
frequently, compared to 43 percent of middle primary students on average internationally (see Exhibit 12.7). 
It is important to look at this data with caution, as the reader could assume that 24 percent of students had 
reported that they were bullied weekly. The Students bullied at school scale is based on individual behaviours, 
and could range from a repeated behaviour experienced on a weekly basis, or potentially many diff erent 
behaviours experienced on separate occasions only once a month, and instigated by diff erent perpetrators.

New Zealand Year 5 students who almost never experienced bullying behaviours had similar science 
achievement, on average, with those who experienced it about monthly, while both had signiﬁ cantly higher 
achievement than those who experienced it about weekly. Internationally, students who experienced bullying 
more frequently had lower science achievement, on average, than those who experienced it less frequently. 
The Republic of Korea, a high-performing country, was an exception, where the frequency of experiencing 
bullying behaviours at school made no diff erence to science achievement results (similar achievement across 
all categories of the scale).
Trends in students’ perceptions about school safety – TIMSS 2010/11 and 2014/15
Questions on students’ perceptions about school safety have evolved since TIMSS was ﬁ rst implemented 
in 1994. Trend comparisons are best made between the TIMSS 2014/15 and 2010/11 cycles, as both cycles 
shared six out of eight statements on bullying behaviours at school, and these statements had the same 
answer options. 
As Exhibit 12.8 shows, there was a signiﬁ cant decrease in the proportion of Year 5 students experiencing 
three out of six bullying behaviours at least once a month or more frequently, when 2014/15 is compared to 
2010/11 results. There were no signiﬁ cant changes in proportions between the two cycles for the rest of the 
statements.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among the New Zealand Year 5 boys and girls
As shown in Exhibit 12.9, more New Zealand Year 5 boys experienced bullying behaviours than Year 5 
girls did. Boys and girls who experienced bullying behaviour on a weekly basis had signiﬁ cantly lower 
achievement than those who experienced it at least monthly or less frequently.

Experiences of bullying behaviours among different ethnic groupings of Year 5 
students
More Pasiﬁ ka students experienced bullying behaviours about weekly when compared with non-Pasiﬁ ka

grouping when the proportions experiencing bullying behaviours were compared.

bullying behaviours about weekly than non-Maori, non-Pasiﬁ ka and non-Other students. The proportion 
of students who were bullied about monthly or less was about the same across all ethnic groupings (see 
Exhibit 12.10).

Experiences of bullying behaviours within the different socio-economic status 
schools
The socio-economic status of students is thought to have a strong relationship with their achievement. 
TIMSS 2014/15 asked school principals to report on the economic composition of their school by estimating 
the proportion of students from economically disadvantaged and economically affl  uent homes. As reported 
in Chapter 7, schools were categorised as being more affl  uent if more than a quarter of their students were 
from economically affl  uent homes, and not more than a quarter were from economically disadvantaged 
homes. More disadvantaged schools had the opposite, more than a quarter of their students were from 
economically disadvantaged homes, and not more than a quarter from economically affl  uent homes. 
The remaining schools were classiﬁ ed as neither more affl  uent nor more disadvantaged.
As shown in Exhibit 12.11, more affl  uent and neither more affl  uent nor more disadvantaged schools had the 
same proportion of students experiencing bullying behaviours. More disadvantaged schools, however, had a 
bigger proportion of students who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly.
When looking at bullying behaviours, after considering the school’s socio-economic status, science 
achievement was signiﬁ cantly lower for students who experienced bullying behaviours about weekly, 
compared to those who experienced them about monthly or less often across the diff erent socio-
economic schools.

116
CHAPTER 13:
Year 5 teachers’ perceptions 
of climate for learning
Key Findings
The majority of New Zealand schools (79% of Year 5 students) had high or very high emphasis 
on academic success, according to teachers. Higher school emphasis on academic success was 
associated with higher science scores, both in New Zealand and internationally.
Half of the Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very satisﬁ ed with their job; 97 
percent of teachers were proud of the work they did. 
Seventy-one percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who felt their school was 
very safe and orderly.
Compared with many other countries, New Zealand teachers perceived more challenges in 
their teaching. They wanted to have more time to assist individual students, and preferred to 
have less administrative tasks. 
Two thirds of Year 5 students studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs 
were very limiting or somewhat limiting their science teaching. These students had lower 
achievement than those who studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs did not 
limit teaching at all.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Within schools and kura, the quality of teaching has the biggest inﬂ uence on student 
achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). However, no matter how highly qualiﬁ ed 
teachers can be, without proper resources and support, and positive working 
conditions, this factor alone may not be suffi  cient in closing the student achievement 
gap. TIMSS 2010/11 results showed higher achievement for schools that provide good 
working conditions for teachers (Hooper et al., 2013). The achievement of important 
academic and social goals is facilitated when the environment is conducive to 
success (Robinson, Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009). Johnson (2006) and Johnson et al. 
(2012) state that “a manageable workload, adequate facilities, and the availability 
of instructional materials are important ingredients to fostering productive working 
conditions and promoting teacher satisfaction” (as cited in Hooper et al., 2013). 
Caprara et al. (2006) attests that a positive school culture and environment can 
lead to greater teacher job satisfaction and self-effi  cacy, which in turn can increase 
student learning (in Hooper et al., 2013). “An orderly environment makes it possible 
for teachers to focus on teaching and students to focus on learning” (Robinson, 
Hohepa, & Lloyd, 2009).
To explore teachers’ perceptions of both the physical and social school 
environments, TIMSS collected data from them on aspects of their schools. 
This also included data on teachers’ job satisfaction, teacher interactions, teachers’ 
perceptions of school safety, limitations to science teaching and challenges facing 
teachers. This chapter presents the ﬁ ndings of these analyses.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

School emphasis on academic success
Middle primary teachers were asked to characterise the diff erent aspects of school life within their school 
that contribute to academic success. The statements varied from teachers, parents and students’ involvement 
in learning and school life, to teacher’s job satisfaction, as listed in Exhibit 13.1. They were given ﬁ ve response 
options: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
Out of all the 18 statements listed, the aspect of schooling rated highly by the most teachers was teachers’ 
expectations for student achievement, with 94 percent of Year 5 students having teachers who felt that 
teachers had very high or high expectations for student achievement within their school. The more highly 
rated statements were generally about teachers’ characteristics and role in learning. On the least reported 
end of the list, about half of Year 5 students’ teachers thought that parental support for student achievement, 
parental commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn, and parental involvement in school activities 
were not high within their school.
The TIMSS 2014/15 teacher questionnaire included 11 new statements that were not asked in previous cycles, 
but seven out of the 18 statements were included in the TIMSS 2010/11 study as well. Except for teachers’ job 
satisfaction, emphasis on these statements has not changed since 2010/11. The proportion of Year 5 students 
whose teachers felt that the teachers within their school were very highly or highly satisﬁ ed with their jobs 
decreased from 81 percent in 2010/11 to 69 percent in 2014/15.

The international researchers combined the responses to 14 out of the 18 statements in the School emphasis 
on academic success scale (see Exhibit 13.2). TIMSS indicators of school emphasis on academic success 
included school administrators’ and teachers’ expectations for successful curriculum implementation 
and student achievement, parental support for student achievement, and the student’s desire to achieve. 
Therefore, the scale didn’t include the statements about teachers’ job satisfaction, clarity of the school’s 
educational objectives, amount of instructional support provided to teachers by school leadership and school 
leadership’s support for teachers’ professional development. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values 
were grouped into three categories: very high; high; and medium emphasis. As the majority of responses 
were very positive, there was no low or very low category in the scale.
As shown in Exhibit 13.2, the majority of New Zealand schools (79%) had high or very high emphasis on 
academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had high or very high 
emphasis on academic success.
Higher school emphasis on academic success reported by the teachers was associated with higher middle 
primary student achievement scores in TIMSS science, both in New Zealand and internationally, even though 
the score diff erence between the students of very high and high emphasis schools was not signiﬁ cant for 
New Zealand. The science achievement diff erence between middle primary students whose schools had very 
high emphasis on academic success, and those whose schools had medium emphasis, was higher in New 
Zealand than on average internationally (50 score points in New Zealand, compared with 31 score points on 
average internationally).

Teachers’ job satisfaction
To better understand teachers’ satisfaction with their jobs, they were asked seven statements about their 
feelings of being a teacher, as shown in Exhibit 13.3. The answer options to the question “How often do 
you feel the following way about being a teacher?” were: very often; often; sometimes; and never or almost 
never. However, due to the very small percentage of never or almost never responses, the graph shows this 
category combined with sometimes as sometimes or less often. 
Overall, New Zealand Year 5 teachers felt very positive about being teachers. Ninety-seven percent of 
Year 5 students had teachers who were very often or often proud of their work. Most teachers also were 
enthusiastic about their job (94% of Year 5 students very often or often) and found their work full of meaning 
and purpose (91% of students very often or often). Less than one percent of teachers replied never or almost 
never to nearly all of the statements in Exhibit 13.3. The statement I am going to continue teaching for as long 
as I can was the least frequently experienced feeling about being a teacher, with ﬁ ve percent of teachers who 
never or almost never felt this way, and with 79 percent, the lowest among all seven statements, who felt it 
very often or often.

On the basis of responses to these statements, the international researchers created a Teacher job 
satisfaction scale. This scale combines all responses into three categories: very satisﬁ ed; satisﬁ ed; and less 
than satisﬁ ed.
As shown in Exhibit 13.4, half of New Zealand Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very 
satisﬁ ed with their jobs, and 43 percent were taught by teachers who were satisﬁ ed. The New Zealand 
proportions were similar to international averages across all levels of job satisfaction. There was not a strong 
relationship between teachers’ job satisfaction and their students’ achievement. Although the graph shows 
small diff erences between the mean achievement scores of New Zealand Year 5 students depending on their 
teachers’ job satisfaction level, these diff erences were not signiﬁ cant. Internationally, many countries had 
the same pattern as New Zealand. Only a few countries like Hong Kong SAR, Spain, Portugal and the United 
Arab Emirates had higher achievement for the students whose teachers were very satisﬁ ed with their job 
than those whose were in other categories, which was the same pattern as with the international averages.

Interactions between teachers
Teachers, as professionals, spend time learning and improving their practice throughout their careers. An 
excellent way to learn is through interactions and collaborations with other teachers. TIMSS asked teachers 
how often they interact with other teachers, with seven types of interaction provided in the questionnaire, 
as shown in Exhibit 13.5. Four possible response options for the frequency of interactions were: very often; 
often; sometimes; and never or almost never. 
The ﬁ rst four types of interactions listed in Exhibit 13.5 were equally common among Year 5 teachers, with 
almost three quarters of students having teachers who reported having these interactions very often or 
often. Visiting another classroom to learn more about teaching was the least frequently practiced interaction 
by a considerable margin. The frequency of teacher interactions appeared to have no link with Year 5 student 
science achievement.

Teachers’ perceptions of school safety and student 
behaviours
To learn what teachers think about their school safety and student behaviour in their schools, teachers of 
Year 5 students were asked to indicate the extent to which they agreed or disagreed with eight statements 
on the general levels of safety they experienced at their schools (see Exhibit 13.6). There were four possible 
response options given: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot. 
Almost all Year 5 students were taught by teachers who agreed (a lot and a little combined) their school 
was a safe place, with 99 percent agreeing with the statement I feel safe at this school. The least agreed 
statement was the students respect school property, with 14 percent of Year 5 students having teachers 
disagree to some extent. Five of the eight statements were asked in the TIMSS 2010/11 cycle as well. 
Comparing the responses to these statements in each cycle shows no signiﬁ cant change in the proportions.

International researchers summarised teachers’ responses to these statements into a scale called the Safe 
and orderly school scale. This scale describes the extent to which teachers felt their school was a safe and 
orderly environment with three value categories: very safe and orderly; safe and orderly; and less than safe 
and orderly. 
As shown in Exhibit 13.7, 71 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who agreed that 
their school was very safe and orderly, which was higher than the international average of 57 percent. 
Internationally, middle primary students had higher achievement in schools where the level of safety was 
higher. In New Zealand, Year 5 students who studied in very safe and orderly schools had higher scores than 
those in other category schools (there was no signiﬁ cant achievement diff erence between safe and less than 
safe and orderly schools).

Challenges facing teachers
To ﬁ nd out about the challenges facing Year 5 teachers, they were asked to rate their agreement with 
the statements listed in Exhibit 13.8 using: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot as 
answer options. The most common challenge that the Year 5 teachers were facing was lack of time to assist 
individual students, with 57 percent of students having teachers agreeing a lot, and 35 percent agreeing 
a little to this statement. Having too much material to cover in class was also among the most commonly 
experienced challenges, with 84 percent of teachers agreeing a lot or a little to this statement. The Year 5 
teachers were less likely to report experiencing too much pressure from parents or too many teaching hours, 
compared to other challenges.

The international researchers combined the responses to these statements into a scale called Challenges 
facing teachers. According to the scale, 14 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who faced 
many challenges in their teaching, while 31 percent faced few challenges (see Exhibit 13.9). Internationally, 
fewer middle primary teachers were facing many challenges in their teaching. Teachers having few or many 
challenges appeared to have a negative relationship with student science achievement in New Zealand, even 
though the connection was not strong. The Year 5 students whose teachers faced many challenges in their 
teaching had signiﬁ cantly higher achievement than those whose teachers faced few challenges, while neither 
were signiﬁ cantly diff erent than those in the some challenges category. In contrast, internationally, the 
relationship was positive and consistent; those with fewer challenges had higher scores than those whose 
teachers were facing more challenges in their teaching.

Limitations to science teaching
In addition to asking about challenges facing teachers, TIMSS asked middle primary teachers to describe to 
what extent the factors listed in Exhibit 13.10 limited science teaching in their classes. Response options were: 
not at all; some; and a lot. 
The most common limitation was students lacking prerequisite knowledge or skills, with 84 percent of Year 
5 students having teachers who thought this factor put some or a lot of limitations on how they taught their 
class. The factors like disruptive students and students suff ering from not enough sleep were also largely 
reported as putting a lot or some limitations on science teaching. Students with physical disabilities seemed 
to be the least limiting factor; 87 percent of Year 5 students had teachers who reported that this did not limit 
their teaching at all. However, the research didn’t collect data on what percentage of teachers had students 
with physical disabilities in their class. The fact that not every class has these types of students might be an 
explanation why this wouldn’t be a limiting factor for many teachers.

Compared to most TIMSS 2014/15 countries, New Zealand had higher proportions of middle primary 
students whose teachers reported that instruction was limited by students suff ering from not enough sleep 
(73% of Year 5 students) and a lack of basic nutrition (45% of students). The United States was the only 
English-speaking country who had more middle primary students suff ering from not enough sleep and 
basic nutrition than New Zealand, while almost all European and Asian countries had fewer students whose 
teachers reported these factors limiting their teaching.
The international researchers combined the responses to six of the statements into a scale called Teaching 
limited by student needs. The statement students with physical disabilities was not included in the scale. 
The scale had three degrees of limitation: not limited; somewhat limited; and very limited.
According to this scale, and similar to the international average (37%), 34 percent of New Zealand Year 5 
students had teachers who reported having no limitation to their science teaching by students’ needs (see 
Exhibit 13.11). Six percent of students studied in an environment where teachers felt their teaching was very 
limited due to students’ needs.
Limitations to teaching appeared to have strong relationship with student science achievement; the more 
teaching was limited by students’ needs, the lower the achievement. A similar pattern was observed for many 
other countries as well, as shown by the international average.

Class size
TIMSS asked teachers about the size of their classes, as larger or smaller classes can inﬂ uence how the 
teacher chooses to teach science topics. At the Year 5 level, composite classes combining diff erent year 
levels are a common feature in New Zealand primary schools.1 The average TIMSS class size for science in 
New Zealand was 27 students in 2014/15, out of which 16 students were at the Year 5 level. The international 
average class size was 25 students. In the majority of countries, average class size ranged from 20 to 30 
students; Chile (32), Morocco (32), Singapore (36) and the Slovak Republic (19) were the few exceptions. 
It is diffi  cult to draw a conclusion about the relationship between class size and achievement. For example, in 
some countries smaller classes tend to be in rural areas where there are fewer resources, and larger classes in 
urban areas with more resources. Remedial classes may also be smaller. However, TIMSS shows that high-
performing Asian countries have relatively larger class sizes (ranging from 25 to 36 in TIMSS 2014/15), while 
most non-Asian top-performing countries tend to have smaller class sizes (between 20 and 27 students in 
TIMSS 2014/15). The New Zealand Year 5 class size has varied over the diff erent TIMSS cycles. It consistently 
decreased from 29 students in 1994/95, to 25 students in 2010/11, and increased to 27 students in 2014/15.
1 
New Zealand is unusual in the prevalence of composite classes. TIMSS 2014/15 data showed only Belgium and Australia in the 46 
country sample had at least 50% composite classes at the middle primary level.

CHAPTER 14:
Year 5 principals’ 
perceptions of climate 
for learning
 Key Findings
The principals were very positive about their teachers, with more than 80 percent of Year 5 
students having principals who rated the teachers’ role in the school’s academic success as 
very high or high. Principals were less positive about parents’ support and involvement than 
about their teachers and students. 
Principals’ perceptions of their school’s academic climate don’t appear to have changed much 
since 2002/03. The only big change is observed on parental support for student achievement, 
with signiﬁ cant drop from 72 percent in 2010/11 to 56 percent in 2014/15.
The principals and teachers had similar views on most aspects of the school’s academic success, 
the only diff erence of opinion was observed on the students’ respect for classmates who excel in 
school, about which the principals were more positive than the teachers.
More New Zealand schools had high or very high emphasis on academic success, compared to 
the international average and to most English-speaking and Asian high-achieving countries. 
Students studying in schools with higher emphasis on academic success had higher science 
achievement scores. 
Compared to other countries, New Zealand principals reported fewer problems with school 
discipline. Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools where 
the principal reported hardly any problems with school discipline. Being in a school with less of 
a problem was associated with higher achievement in science, on average. There has been no 
change in the level of discipline in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Principals’ perceptions of climate for learning
Principals of Year 5 students were asked how they would characterise thirteen aspects of life at their school, 
listed in Exhibit 14.1. The response options were: very high; high; medium; low; and very low.
The statements the principals were most positive about were mainly related to teachers, with more than 
80 percent of Year 5 students having principals who indicated these aspects were very high or high. The 
majority of principals indicated that teachers' expectations for student achievement (90% of students) and 
teachers' understanding of the school's curricular goals (89% of students) at their school were very high or 
high. Other statements about teachers had slightly lower proportions, with the lowest being teachers’ ability 
to inspire students (84%).
Principals were also very positive about students’ respect for classmates who excel at school, with 88 
percent of Year 5 students having principals who indicated this was very high or high. However, they were 
less positive about other student related aspects like students' desire to do well at school (80% of students) 
and students' ability to reach school's academic goals (75% of students). 
Compared to their responses about teachers and students, responses by the principals to parental support 
and involvement were less positive. Parental commitment to ensure that students are ready to learn, the 
lowest of the ﬁ ve statements, had only about half of Year 5 students whose school principal indicated 
this aspect was very high or high. Of the ﬁ ve statements, more principals were positive about parental 
expectations for student achievement, with 71 percent of Year 5 students having principals who indicated 
this was very high or high.
Principals may not be part of classroom teaching, but they have a strong indirect 
inﬂ uence on education achievement. Hitt and Tucker (2015) established ﬁ ve essential 
broad domains of eff ective leadership practices that positively align with student 
achievement: establishing and conveying the vision; facilitating a high-quality 
learning experience for students; building professional capacity; creating an eff ective 
and supportive environment for learning; and connecting with external partners. 
As part of the Investing in Educational Success programme, the Ministry of Education 
introduced Principal Recruitment Allowance to help New Zealand’s most high-need 
schools and kura attract highly-eff ective principals who can provide the leadership 
needed to lift student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). To be able to 
eff ectively lead, principals need to see the “bigger picture”, and to have knowledge 
and insight into the complex processes taking place in their school (Ministry of 
Education, 2008). 
TIMSS collected data on principals’ perceptions of their school environment, in 
conjunction with other factors that contribute to eff ective schools. This chapter will 
present ﬁ ndings on principals’ perceptions of their school in relation to the students’ 
achievement.

Year 5 teachers were asked the same questions as principals, and responded similarly (see Exhibit 14.2).

characterisation of the aspect, the higher the science achievement. The parental support and involvement 
statements, and the statements about students, followed this general pattern. However, the principals’ 
responses to the statements about teachers didn’t appear to have a signiﬁ cant relationship with student 
achievement, except for teachers’ degree of success in implementing the school’s curriculum. The students in 
schools where principals responded very high or high to this statement had higher achievement than those 
where principals responded medium.
