The international researchers combined the principals’ responses to these statements in the School emphasis 
on academic success scale (see Exhibit 14.3). However, this scale is diff erent from the same named scale 
created on the basis of teachers’ responses (see Exhibit 13.2), as the latter included one extra statement, 
collaboration between school leadership and teachers to plan instruction, which was not asked to the 
principals. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values were grouped into three categories: very high; high; 
and medium emphasis. As principals had responded very positively to these statements, there was no low or 
very low category on this scale. 
As shown in Exhibit 14.3, the majority of New Zealand schools (79%) had high or very high emphasis on 
academic success. Compared to the international average, more New Zealand schools had high and very 
high emphasis on academic success. Thirteen out of 47 participating TIMSS 2014/15 countries, including 
high achievers like Norway, Belgium (Flemish), Finland and the Netherlands, had one percent or less of 
their middle primary students studying in schools with very high emphasis on academic success, with the 
majority of students being in schools with medium emphasis on academic success. New Zealand had more 
schools in the very high emphasis on academic success category than most English-speaking and Asian 
high-achieving countries. 
The general pattern, as observed both by the international average and in New Zealand, was that the higher 
on the scale the emphasis by the principal, the higher the associated achievement score in TIMSS for science. 
In New Zealand, there was a diff erence of 59 scale score points for science, on average, between those 
students whose principals rated very high on the scale and those who rated medium, while it was 34 scale 
score points on the international average.

desire to do well in school (signiﬁ cant drop from 88 to 80% in student proportion) and parental support for 
student achievement (signiﬁ cant drop from 72 to 56% in student proportion) in their school as being very 
high or high.

Principals’ perceptions of school discipline and student 
behaviours
As school leader, the principal plays a major role in building and sustaining an eff ective learning 
environment and a positive school climate. TIMSS 2014/15 asked the school principals about how much 
of a problem a series of behaviours occurring in their school were to the school order (see Exhibit 14.5). 
Principals expressed the extent to which these behaviours were a problem among Year 5 students in their 
school with one of the following response options: not a problem; minor problem; moderate problem; or 
serious problem. The majority of Year 5 students attended schools where these behaviours were perceived 
by the principal to be a minor problem at the most. Few students had principals that acknowledged any 
of these behaviours as posing a serious problem in their school. The most common behaviours causing 
moderate and serious problems for school discipline were arriving late at school, absenteeism and 
classroom disturbance, all with less than 10 percent of Year 5 students.

To summarise the extent to which school discipline aff ects student learning, the TIMSS international team 
created the School discipline problems scale, based on principals’ views on the extent to which the ten 
behaviours listed in Exhibit 14.5 occurred among middle primary students in their schools. To report the scale 
in a meaningful way, values on the scale were grouped into three categories: moderate to severe problems; 
minor problems; and hardly any problems.
Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools whose principals indicated 
that there were hardly any problems with school discipline, and only one percent attended schools where 
there were moderate to severe problems. New Zealand principals were more positive than on average 
internationally (61% had hardly any problems and 9% had moderate to severe problems, on average, 
internationally). All of the English-speaking countries who participated in TIMSS 2014/15 had a proportion 
of students similar to, or above, the international average in the hardly any problems category, as did a 
number of the higher-achieving countries. This ﬁ nding appears to contrast with student reports of bullying 
behaviours at school; New Zealand was one of the countries with the most Year 5 students regularly 
experiencing bullying behaviours (see Exhibit 12.7).

Overall, being in a school with less of a discipline problem was associated with higher achievement in 
science, both in New Zealand and on average internationally. However, this was not consistent over all 
countries; several countries showed no signiﬁ cant variation in achievement scores across the categories. 
Japan, the Czech Republic, and Portugal were among these countries. In some countries, such as Georgia, 
middle primary students whose school was categorised as having moderate to severe problems had higher 
average achievement scores than those in the minor problems category, and similar to those in the hardly 
any problems category. For some other countries, like Slovenia for example, there was a higher score for 
those with moderate to severe problems over those in the hardly any problems category.
Trends in principals’ perceptions of school discipline
Even though the statements about school discipline were asked in previous TIMSS cycles as well, in 2002/03 
and 2006/07 this question was about the frequency of a series of problem behaviours occurring in the 
schools, and how severe a problem these behaviours were. In 2010/11, the question was adjusted to only ask 
how much of a problem these behaviours were in the school, with diff erent answer options compared to the 
previous two cycles. Due to these diff erences, we are able to compare the results across only TIMSS 2014/15 
and 2010/11. The comparison of the individual statements and the School discipline problems scale results 
shows no change in the level of discipline in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11.

CHAPTER 15:
Year 5 parents’ perceptions 
of climate for learning
 Key Findings
Parents had very positive perceptions of their child’s school. Students whose parents were 
satisﬁ ed with their child’s school had higher science achievement than students whose parents 
were less than satisﬁ ed. There was no diff erence in achievement for students whose parents 
were very satisﬁ ed and other groups. 
Nearly all parents (97%) agreed that their child’s school provided a safe environment for 
learning. 
A higher percentage of parents felt that schools were doing a good job in helping the students 
to become better in mathematics or reading than in science. Compared to 2010/11, in 2014/15 
more parents agreed that their child’s school does a good job in helping him/her become better 
in science.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

The four year plan of the Ministry of Education highlights that educational success 
depends on the eff orts and support of not just schools, teachers and principals, but 
also parents, whānau and communities. The action plan places important focus on 
working more closely with parents, family and whānau, communities and employers 
to raise student achievement (Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Research shows that, one of the attributes of a well-functioning school is having a 
mutually trustful relationship with parents, and their active involvement in school life 
and learning. Schools that encourage and welcome parental involvement are more 
likely to have highly involved parents than schools that do not make an eff ort to keep 
parents informed and participating (Jeynes, 2005 in Hooper et al., 2013). Students 
whose parents are actively involved in their school tend to have a better academic 
performance, fewer behavioural problems and overall a positive attitude toward 
school (Henderson & Berla, 1994, Taylor et al., 2000; Jeynes, 2005; Dearing et al., 
2006; Jeynes, 2007; Hill & Tyson, 2009).
To better understand the eff ects of the home, TIMSS 2014/15 collected a range of 
data from student’s parents or caregivers. Parents’ perceptions of school climate and 
performance are discussed in this chapter.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS

Parents’ perceptions of school performance
Parents of students who participated in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe how much they agreed or 
disagreed with a series of statements about the diff erent aspects of the school their child attended (list in 
Exhibit 15.1). The response options were: agree a lot; agree a little; disagree a little; and disagree a lot. Only 56 
percent of New Zealand Year 5 parents returned their completed questionnaire, so the results in this section 
should be read with this in mind. 
Overall, parents who responded to the questionnaire were very positive about their child’s school. As 
shown in Exhibit 15.1, over 90 percent of parents agreed with ﬁ ve out of eight statements. Somewhat lower 
percentages of parents agreed that their child’s school does a good job informing them of his/her progress 
(87%), and promotes high academic expectations (86%). Parents’ perceptions of school safety were very 
positive, with 97 percent of students’ parents agreeing that their child’s school provides a safe environment. 
A higher percentage of parents agreed that the school was doing a good job in helping their child become 
better in mathematics or reading (90% and 92% respectively) than helping their child become better in 
science (81%). A similar pattern was observed in TIMSS 2010/11 which may reﬂ ect that there is still strong 
focus on literacy and numeracy in schools. However, compared to 2010/11, more parents agreed that their 
child’s school does a good job in helping him/her become better in science in 2014/15. The proportions 
remained unchanged for all other statements.

Responses to the statements in Exhibit 15.1 were combined by the international researchers to form the 
Parents’ perception of school performance scale. As shown in Exhibit 15.2, 63 percent of New Zealand 
Year 5 students had parents who were very satisﬁ ed with the school their child was attending, and only six 
percent were less than satisﬁ ed. Compared to the international average, New Zealand had slightly more 
students whose parents were very satisﬁ ed about their child’s school performance (63% compared to 59% 
on international average). However, there were some big diff erences across some of the TIMSS countries; 
among 44 countries that had data available, proportions on the very satisﬁ ed category ranged from seven 
percent (Japan) to 91 percent of students (Kazakhstan). Australia, the Netherlands, Norway, England and the 
United States had data available for less than 50 percent of middle primary students, and were therefore not 
included in the analysis.

CHAPTER 16:
School resources for 
Year 5 students
 Key Findings
Teachers most commonly reported lack of technological resources, lack of support for using 
technology, and inadequate workspace for teachers as moderate or serious problems at their 
school. New Zealand Year 5 teachers reported fewer problems, in general, than many of their 
international counterparts.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of technologically competent staff  hindered 
instruction at their school. Many principals also felt that a lack of teachers with a specialisation 
in science, computer software, and science equipment and materials for experiments hindered 
science instruction. The less aff ected the principal felt instruction at the school was by a 
shortage of science resources, the higher the science achievement scores. 
The perceived impact of the lack of science-related resources was much higher than 
mathematical resources.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Quality and availability of school buildings and resources, 
according to teachers
Teachers in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to describe the extent to which various issues created problems 
for them, as listed in Exhibit 16.1. They were given four response options: not a problem; minor problem; 
moderate problem; and serious problem.
Over half of Year 5 students had teachers who identiﬁ ed inadequate workspaces, inadequate technological 
resources and support for using technology as the issues they faced in their school (minor, moderate, or 
serious problems). The least problematic of these issues, in the opinions of the Year 5 teachers, were the 
cleanliness of their classrooms (not a problem for 79% of students), maintenance work on classrooms (61% 
not a problem) and not having adequate instructional materials and supplies (60% not a problem).

The quality and nature of the physical environment of schools and resources have 
been thought to have a signiﬁ cant inﬂ uence on teacher attitudes, behaviours and 
performance, which have been directly linked to student outcomes and achievement 
(Dawson & Parker, 1998; Schneider, 2002; Buckley, Schneider, & Shang, 2004). 
Roberts (1999) found that achievement in science was better when students had 
instruction using a variety of hands-on activities. 
In this chapter, we explore the physical environment of our schools and resources 
to gain an understanding of how the teaching environment and resource shortages 
potentially impact teaching and science achievement in TIMSS. Information on these 
has been obtained from the contextual questionnaires ﬁ lled in by teachers and 
principals in participating schools.

The international researchers combined the responses to the statements in the Problems with school 
conditions and resources scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values on the scale were grouped 
into three categories: moderate to severe problems; minor problems; and hardly any problems. 
As Exhibit 16.2 shows, New Zealand teachers were relatively positive about their working conditions, with a 
small proportion reporting moderate to severe problems with school conditions and resources, compared 
with teachers from other countries. Although New Zealand teachers were not as positive as teachers from 
England, Australia, Hong Kong SAR and Singapore, fewer New Zealand teachers were negative about their 
working conditions compared with their counterparts in some high-achieving countries. 
According to the international average, the more positive teachers were on the scale (that is the less likely 
they were to report on any problem), the higher the average science achievement of middle primary students 
(see Exhibit 16.3). However, in New Zealand, there was no signiﬁ cant diff erence in science achievement 
between those who reported moderate to severe problems, minor problems or hardly any problems.

Impact of shortages of resources, according to principals
Principals were asked to rate if their school’s capacity to provide instruction was aff ected by a shortage or 
inadequacy of general and science resources, as listed in Exhibit 16.3. This question was asked diff erently 
from the question asked of teachers, with the options being: a lot; some; a little; or not at all. Of the 
resources listed, the ones most commonly seen as having an impact on science instruction (a lot, some and 
a little combined) were a lack of teachers with a specialisation in science, computer software, and science 
equipment and materials for experiments. As shown in chapter 10, fewer New Zealand Year 5 teachers had 
a specialisation in science when compared with many other countries. Of the general resources, a lack of 
technologically competent staff , resources for students with disabilities, and computers for teaching and 
learning were the most common resources indicated as hindering instruction. Although there were a few 
changes to this question since TIMSS 2010/11, the lack of technologically competent staff  and computers for 
instruction were also most commonly reported as issues in that cycle. The perceived impact of the lack of 
science-related resources was much higher than for mathematics resources. This pattern was also observed 
in 2010/11 (see Caygill, Kirkham, & Marshall, 2013a; Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016).

Principals were also asked about resources for mathematics instruction - see Caygill, Singh, & Hanlar, 2016, for these results.
The international researchers combined principals’ responses to the statements in Exhibit 16.3 into the 
Instruction aff ected by science resource shortages scale. To report the scale in a meaningful way, values on 
the scale were grouped into three categories: not aff ected; aff ected; and aff ected a lot. As Exhibit 16.4 shows, 
26 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were in schools where principals had indicated that resource 
shortages had not aff ected science instruction. Seventy-four percent of Year 5 students were in schools 
where instruction was somewhat aff ected by shortages in science resources, and one percent of students 
were aff ected a lot. These proportions are not signiﬁ cantly diff erent from 2010/11 (see Caygill, Kirkham, & 
Marshall, 2013a). 
A similar proportion of middle primary students (25%) were in schools internationally, on average, where 
principals felt that instruction was not at all aff ected by science resource shortages. Principals in the 
Republic of Korea reported as high as 76 percent of students not being aff ected by resource shortages. 
On the other hand, Hong Kong SAR had a smaller proportion of middle primary students, compared to 
New Zealand, where principals felt that instruction was not aff ected by science resource shortages. In 
contrast, Singapore, a high performing country, had nine percent of students that were aff ected a lot by 
science resource shortages.

The international average shows that, in general, the less aff ected the school was by a shortage of science 
resources, the higher the achievement scores (see Exhibit 16.4). This general ﬁ nding held true for most 
countries, including New Zealand, but the Republic of Korea, Singapore and Japan were exceptions. This 
implies that, in general, resourcing is important in ensuring good achievement outcomes. However, as is 
indicated by principals, the teachers need to know how to use the resources, and to use them appropriately, 
to ensure they have a positive impact on the student outcomes.

Availability of laboratories
Principals were asked if the school had a science laboratory that can be used by Year 5 students. No 
deﬁ nition was given in the question of what was meant by a science laboratory. Six percent of Year 5 
students in New Zealand attended schools with a science laboratory that could be used by Year 5 students, 
compared to the international average of 38 percent. These proportions have not changed signiﬁ cantly since 
2010/11.
On average internationally, and in New Zealand, the science achievement of students in schools where there 
were science laboratories available for middle primary students was higher than those in schools without. 
Like all resources, having a science laboratory may not make any diff erent to achievement unless it is used to 
enhance the teaching that happens outside the laboratory.

Access to specialists
New Zealand principals were asked three questions about the availability of assistance and experts. 
Speciﬁ cally, the three questions were:
 »
Do teachers usually have assistance available when students are conducting science experiments?
 »
Do teachers at your school have access to a science expert or science education specialist to help them 
when they are preparing a new topic in science?
 »
In the last two years, has your school been involved in a science project(s) where you partnered with 
someone outside your school to help students learn speciﬁ c science concepts?
One in ten students were in schools where teachers usually have assistance when students are conducting 
experiments. Just under one-quarter of students (24%) were in schools where there was access to a science 
expert within the school, and another 10 percent where access to someone outside their school was available. 
One-third of students were in schools that had been involved in a science project where they partnered with 
someone from outside the school to help students learn speciﬁ c science concepts. 
Some of the partner projects involved locally available experts in the high schools or colleges, the 
universities, scientists (including parents of children in the school), and local industries. Others included 
CREST science, Science Alive, the House of Science, a science roadshow, and science fairs. These activities 
ﬁ t with the vision set out in A Nation of Curious Minds (Ministry of Business, Innovation and Employment and 
Ministry of Education, 2014)

CHAPTER 17:
Student abilities at 
school entry
 Key Findings
Students who had attended an early childhood facility for more than one year had higher 
science achievement by Year 5 than those who had attended for less time, or not at all.
Parents’ reports show that many students begin school already having some literacy and 
numeracy abilities, and that this early preparation has a positive eff ect on their achievement 
later on.
According to principals’ estimates of the literacy abilities of students when they began school, 
science achievement at Year 5 was higher in schools where the cohort had more literacy 
abilities when they were new entrants. 
In general, more affl  uent schools were more likely to report higher proportions of able students 
in their school intake.
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

Pre-primary education (or early childhood education, ECE) builds on early learning 
children may receive at home, and helps prepare them for a future of learning, 
including primary school. As mentioned in Chapter 8, home circumstances inﬂ uence 
the achievement of school-age children. In addition, the beneﬁ ts of quality early 
childhood education have long been acknowledged in numerous publications. 
There is a wide range of early childhood education services available to children in 
New Zealand, and government subsidies enable all children to have access to them.
Although not compulsory in New Zealand, the Government aims to increase 
participation in ECE (Ministry of Education, 2012). Partnering Up was established 
in 2010 with the aims of raising overall participation in ECE. The programme has 
been hailed a success, reporting in December 2015 that 96 percent of children had 
participated in ECE prior to starting school. In particular, more Māori and Pasiﬁ ka 
children attended ECE in 2015 (in December, 94% and 92% respectively) than in 2010 
(in June, 90% and 86% respectively – Ministry of Education, 2016). 
Early childhood education in New Zealand takes place in a mixture of teacher-led 
and parent-led services. The early childhood curriculum framework, Te Whāriki, is 
used by most New Zealand ECE services to guide children’s learning opportunities. 
The framework is made up of ﬁ ve strands, well-being, belonging, contribution, 
communication, and exploration; and the curriculum speciﬁ es particular learning 
outcomes associated with the goals of each strand. Learning outcomes most 
relevant to the early scientiﬁ c skills examined in TIMSS are found across the ﬁ ve 
strands of Te Whāriki. In particular, in the exploration strand, it is intended that 
children learn strategies for active investigation, thinking, and reasoning, and in the 
well-being strand, it is intended that students develop consciousness of one’s place 
in the environment, fostering curiosity and scientiﬁ c understanding (Ministry of 
Education, 1996). 
This chapter will examine early childhood education in the New Zealand context, 
along with principals’ and parents’ responses when asked to estimate the abilities of 
children (with a focus on literacy and numeracy) when they ﬁ rst began school.

Early childhood education in New Zealand
The Early learning survey administered to parents or caregivers of Year 5 students in TIMSS 2014/15 
requested information on the length of time spent in ECE prior to starting school, the types of ECE 
establishments attended the year prior to starting school, and the hours spent at these each week. Note that 
only 56 percent of New Zealand parents returned the questionnaire, so values refer to only these students 
and their parents. As shown in Exhibit 17.1, over half of Year 5 students (56%) had participated in early 
childhood education as pre-schoolers for three years or more. The students with three years or more in ECE 
had a signiﬁ cantly higher mean science achievement score (534) than the relatively small proportion (5%) of 
Year 5 students who had not participated at all (469). Higher science achievement scores were associated 
with more time spent in ECE for both New Zealand Year 5 students and on average internationally. This 
ﬁ nding suggests that some early childhood education is better than none or very little.

in early childhood education, compared to middle primary students on average internationally

In most TIMSS countries, pre-primary education or early childhood education (ECE) is voluntary, although 
participation rates are high. In other TIMSS countries, one year of pre-primary education is compulsory 
(e.g. Denmark and Hungary). The length of participation in ECE varies across countries, which may be 
inﬂ uenced by their various school starting ages. This means that the context for early childhood education 
within each country needs to be considered before making cross-country comparisons. For example, 
countries like Denmark have a school starting age of 7, so are more likely to report higher proportions 
of children attending ECE for three years or more, compared to Northern Ireland, where the majority of 
children start primary school at the age of 4. Therefore, it is probably more beneﬁ cial to look at how ECE 
participation is related to achievement within a country (Chamberlain, 2012).

TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5

»

Comparisons between the 2010/11 and 2014/15 TIMSS cycles on the changes in length of ECE participation 
cannot be made as the categories used in each cycle diff er greatly. It is worth noting, however, that both 
cycles observed a general trend where more time spent in ECE before primary school was associated with 
higher science achievement. 
The number of hours in early childhood education prior to starting school
New Zealand Year 5 students participating in TIMSS 2014/15 probably started school sometime over the 
period June 2010 through to May 2011, and would have been eligible for 20 Hours ECE, launched in July 2007. 
This policy enables all three-, four- and ﬁ ve-year olds to receive up to 20 hours a week of free early childhood 
education in all teacher-led services and some kōhanga reo (Ministry of Education, 2016). Children from low-
income families are also eligible for extra subsidies (Ministry of Education, 2016). As mentioned previously, 
parents or caregivers of Year 5 students in TIMSS 2014/15 were asked to estimate the number of hours their 
child had attended an early childhood education facility in the year prior to starting school. Of those whose 
parents/caregivers responded, almost two-thirds of Year 5 students had spent more than 20 hours per week 
in ECE in the year immediately before they had started school. More hours spent each week in ECE services 
were associated with higher science scores, on average (see Exhibit 17.2).

There are various types of ECE services available in New Zealand, and many children attend more than 
one type of service. Exhibit 17.3 shows a breakdown of the types of ECE services Year 5 students attended 
the year immediately before starting school. Of the total number of students whose parents or caregivers 
indicated they had attended ECE, around 36 percent of those students had attended more than one ECE 
service in the year before starting school. It is important to note that playgroups, although recognised as a 
form of ECE in New Zealand, are generally not viewed as ECE in the international context.

Estimate of abilities at school entry 
Parents’ reports
Parents or caregivers were asked to estimate their child’s early literacy and numeracy skills when they ﬁ rst 
began primary school. When reading this section, remember that only 56 percent of parents or caregivers 
returned questionnaires. Parents were not asked about their children’s science ability, but literacy and 
numeracy skills are both important skills which will be useful when learning science. Parents or caregivers of 
New Zealand students were asked whether their child could: recognise most of the letters of the alphabet; 
read some words; read sentences; read a story; write letters of the alphabet; and write some words. The 
results of these questions were summarised into a measure of how well children could do literacy tasks when 
they began school. As shown in Exhibit 17.4, over half of New Zealand Year 5 parents thought their children 
could do the early literacy tasks moderately well or very well (54%). Almost half of parents or caregivers who 
responded thought that their children could not do these tasks well (46%).

Parents or caregivers were also asked about students’ numeracy skills upon beginning school. These tasks 
were: recognise written numbers; count by himself/herself; write numbers; do simple addition; do simple 
subtraction; count money; and measure lengths or heights. The results of these questions were summarised 
into a measure of how well children could do numeracy tasks when they began school. As Exhibit 17.5 shows, 
similar to reports on literacy skills, over half of New Zealand Year 5 parents thought their children could do 
the early numeracy tasks moderately well or very well (54%). The proportion of students thought not able to 
do the early numeracy tasks well was the same as for the literacy tasks (46% not well for both). 
As Exhibit 17.4 and Exhibit 17.5 show, students that were deemed able to do early literacy and numeracy tasks 
very well before starting school had signiﬁ cantly higher mean science achievement scores compared to Year 
5 students who could do these tasks moderately well or not well at all when they started school. Parents’ or 
caregivers’ reports on whether students could perform early literacy and numeracy tasks when they began 
primary school illustrates that the advantage of early abilities appears to still be evident in Year 5.

Principals’ reports
Principals were asked to estimate what proportions of students in their school could do a range of early 
literacy and numeracy skills when they began primary school. Literacy skills were based on the following 
statements: recognise most letters of the alphabet; read some words; read sentences; write letters of 
the alphabet; write some words; construct sentences orally; and retell and sequence story events. These 
statements are similar, but not the same, as those asked of the parents. As Exhibit 17.6 shows, the most 
common literacy skill for new entrants was the ability to construct sentences orally. Sixty-seven percent of 
students attended schools where at least a quarter of students could construct sentences orally by the time 
they started school. The next most common skills were recognising most letters of the alphabet and writing 
letters of the alphabet. The least common skills were the reading abilities and writing words. 
As Exhibit 17.6 shows, higher science achievement is observed for schools where more than half of students 
have the speciﬁ c literacy skills, compared to schools where fewer students have those speciﬁ c literacy skills 
when they start their ﬁ rst year of school.

Principals were also asked about students’ numeracy skills upon beginning school: count up to 100 or higher; 
recognise written numbers from 1-10; recognise written numbers higher than 10; write numbers from 1-10; 
do simple addition; and do simple subtraction. The most common numeracy skill for new entrants was the 
ability to recognise written numbers from 1 to 10. Fewer new entrants could do the other tasks; a majority 
of New Zealand Year 5 students were in schools where less than a quarter of their peers could count up to 
100, recognise written numbers higher than 10, write all ten numbers from 1 to 10 or do simple addition or 
subtraction prior to school entry. Like literacy skills, higher science achievement was observed for schools 
where more than half of students had these numeracy skills, compared to schools where fewer students 
began school with these speciﬁ c numeracy skills. 
Responses to literacy and numeracy abilities were aggregated to produce an indicator of school readiness. 
As mentioned previously, there is substantial variation in the average age of entry into primary schools and 
prior educational experiences across countries, which need to be considered when making cross-country 
comparisons. New Zealand students typically begin primary school on, or soon after, their ﬁ fth birthday. 
The average age of school entry is 6 years or older for Singapore, the Republic of Korea, Hong Kong SAR, 
Chinese Taipei and Japan, around 5 years for Australia, and 4 years for Northern Ireland. It is interesting to 
note that high performing countries which have the same school starting age shared similar proportions 
of literacy and numeracy skills amongst students entering schools. Similarly, Australia and New Zealand, 
where students start school at the age of 5, shared almost similar proportions (see Exhibit 17.7). Within New 
Zealand, schools where less than a quarter of Year 5 students were estimated to have had these early literacy 
and numeracy skills at school entry had lower science achievement, on average, than those where a higher 
proportion of students started school with these skills.

Early literacy and numeracy abilities and school socio-
economic composition
Schools that were more economically disadvantaged were more likely to have a low proportion of their 
students having literacy and numeracy skills at school entry than schools from more affl  uent communities 
(see Exhibit 17.8). For more information on this categorisation, see Chapter 7. Among the more disadvantaged 
schools in New Zealand, the majority of principals (82% of students in such schools) reported that less than 
25 percent of students who entered their schools had early literacy and numeracy abilities. No schools in this 
category reported that 75 percent or more of their students entered their school with these skills.

These ﬁ ndings appear to reinforce the view that the literacy and numeracy skills that children begin school 
with inﬂ uence later achievement. The diff erences in achievement at Year 5, demonstrated in Chapter 7, 
among schools of diff ering affl  uence may be partially explained by the fact that these students started 
school with diff ering abilities, and these diff erences persisted. This ﬁ nding particularly highlights the 
importance of placing emphasis on early childhood education.

159
Conclusion
Science achievement in TIMSS 2014/15
The average (mean) science achievement of New Zealand Year 5 students in 2014/15 was the same as 
that of the students 20 years ago, but was signiﬁ cantly higher than 2010/11. New Zealand Year 5 students’ 
mean science achievement was signiﬁ cantly higher than 14 countries, but lower than the mean score of 30 
countries, including all the other predominantly English-speaking countries who participated.
Was our average achievement low compared with other countries because the test was inappropriate for 
New Zealand students? When the TIMSS test was compared to curriculum expectations for Year 5 students, 
there were questions considered more advanced than our curriculum. However, when the test was reduced 
to contain only those questions appropriate to New Zealand Year 5 students, the average student got around 
half the items correct. The appropriateness of the questions was judged against the curriculum and science 
resources available on Te kete ipurangi (scienceonline.tki.org.nz). By the end of their ﬁ fth year of schooling, 
New Zealand students are expected to be achieving at early level 3 in the curriculum (see, for example, 
Ministry of Education, 2009). According to their teachers, many Year 5 students were working at level 2 of 
the curriculum by the end of the year, rather than level 3. Perhaps not surprisingly, those students working at 
higher levels had higher science achievement.
Strengths and weaknesses in Year 5 science achievement 
New Zealand Year 5 students consistently, since 2006/07, show greatest strength in Earth science and life 
science, compared to physical science. The life science score signiﬁ cantly increased between 2010/11 and 
2014/15. Life science was also the area of the curriculum most likely to be covered by their teachers in the 
lessons. Overall, New Zealand teachers felt slightly less prepared to teach physical science topics than life 
and Earth science topics. 
New Zealand Year 5 students did signiﬁ cantly better at reasoning, compared with knowing and applying 
their knowledge. There has been an improvement in the averages for both knowing and reasoning between 
2010/11 and 2014/15. New Zealand Year 5 teachers were also less conﬁ dent to do science teaching activities 
than mathematics. Five out of 10 science teaching activities had less than half of the Year 5 teachers who 
reported having high or very high conﬁ dence to do these activities. New Zealand teachers expressed less 
conﬁ dence in science teaching than many of their peers internationally.
Equity among Year 5 students
“Over many years, the New Zealand education system has been characterised by relatively high levels of 
inequality” (Ministry of Education, 2016). One way to examine equity is to look at the gap between the 
high and low performers. The gap (range) for Year 5 students in 2014/15 has not changed since 2010/11, 
but is not as wide as it was in 1994/95 and 1998/99. Six percent of New Zealand Year 5 students were 
classiﬁ ed as advanced performers (reached the advanced benchmark), while 12 percent of students were 
classiﬁ ed as below low performers, who did not perform simple science tasks. Compared with other 
countries, New Zealand was around the middle when considering the proportion of advanced performers, 
but had a high proportion of lower performers. High performing countries had at least a ﬁ fth of their 
middle primary students classiﬁ ed as advanced performers, and four percent or fewer classiﬁ ed as below 
low performers.
The proportions of Year 5 students reaching each classiﬁ cation in 2014/15 was signiﬁ cantly lower than 
2002/03 (the TIMSS cycle with the best results for New Zealand Year 5 students). The proportion of Year 
5 students classiﬁ ed as intermediate or high performers, has increased signiﬁ cantly between 2010/11 and 
2014/15.
Back to Contents
»

In general, the diff erence between girls and boys in science has not been marked in recent years. Year 5 boys 
and girls had the same science achievement, on average, but boys had a wider range than girls. Boys and 
girls in 2014/15 both had signiﬁ cantly higher science achievement than 20 years ago.
Equity among ethnic groupings remains a concern. Nearly one-quarter of all Year 5 students identiﬁ ed 
themselves as belonging in more than one ethnic grouping; the groupings were Māori, Pasiﬁ ka, Pākehā/
European, Asian, and Other. There was a wide variation in science achievement within ethnic groupings, 
with advanced achievers and very low achievers in all ethnic groupings. Māori had lower average science 
achievement than non-Māori. Similarly, Pasiﬁ ka students had lower average science achievement than non-
Pasiﬁ ka. There was evidence that this achievement gap narrows when socio-economic factors are taken 
into account.
The relationship between home life and schooling outcomes can be examined in TIMSS. Children in homes 
with many resources for learning had higher science achievement, on average, than those whose homes had 
fewer resources. Students who often did early literacy and numeracy learning activities with their parents 
(according to the parents) had higher science achievement, on average, than students who did so less often. 
Students who had attended an early childhood facility for more than 1 year had higher science achievement 
by Year 5 than those who had attended for less time, or not at all.
It was clear from the TIMSS results that some schools face more barriers to helping their Year 5 students 
achieve than others. Science achievement was higher, on average, for students in schools with more affl  uent 
students than those in schools with more economically disadvantaged students. The diff erence in science 
achievement between these two groupings within New Zealand was higher than most other countries in the 
TIMSS study.
Parents reports show that many students begin school already having some literacy and numeracy abilities, 
and that this early preparation has a positive eff ect on their achievement later on. According to principals’ 
estimates of the literacy abilities of students when they began school, science achievement at Year 5 was 
higher in schools where the cohort had more literacy abilities when they were new entrants. In general, more 
affl  uent schools were more likely to report higher proportions of able students in their school intake.
Resources available at school could help off set the lack of resources at home. TIMSS asked about the level 
to which lack of resources at school was a problem. Teachers most commonly reported lack of technological 
resources, lack of support for using technology, and inadequate workspace for teachers as moderate or 
serious problems at their school. New Zealand Year 5 teachers reported fewer problems, in general, than 
many of their international counterparts. Interestingly, at the Year 5 level, New Zealand had the highest 
proportion of students with computers available for use during science lessons, compared to all other 
countries.
Principals most commonly reported a lack of technologically competent staff  hindered instruction at their 
school. Many principals also felt that a lack of teachers with a specialisation in science, computer software, 
and science equipment and materials for experiments hindered science instruction. The less aff ected 
the principal felt instruction at the school was by a shortage of science resources, the higher the science 
achievement scores. The perceived impact of the lack of science-related resources was much higher than for 
the mathematical resources.
Student and parental attitudes to learning science
To what extent do parent and student attitudes relate to science achievement? Positive parental attitudes 
towards mathematics and science were associated with higher science achievement.
The average (mean) science achievement score was higher for the group of Year 5 students who read every 
day or almost every day, and declined as the amount of time spent on reading reduced. New Zealand Year 
5 students spent more time on sports as an out of school activity, compared to other activities which were 
culturally focussed. Tutoring in science was less common compared to mathematics internationally, and even 
less so in New Zealand. Only two percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had parents report that their child 
attended tutoring for science.
160
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
»
Back to Contents

Nine out of ten New Zealand Year 5 students liked learning science, which was similar to the international 
average. Many more Year 5 students liked learning science than felt conﬁ dent in their science abilities – 58 
percent very much liked learning science, while 24 percent were very conﬁ dent in science. Although, three 
quarters of New Zealand Year 5 students were conﬁ dent in science, New Zealand had more not conﬁ dent 
and less very conﬁ dent students in science, compared with international averages. Students had higher 
science achievement, on average, if they were conﬁ dent in their science ability, or liked science. The science 
achievement of students had a stronger relationship with their conﬁ dence in science than how much they 
liked learning science.
There was no disproportionate representation of any ethnic grouping on how much the students liked 
learning science. However, the Māori and Pasiﬁ ka groupings stood out with a bigger proportion of not 
conﬁ dent students than very conﬁ dent students, while the Pākehā/European grouping had a bigger 
proportion of very conﬁ dent students than not conﬁ dent students.
Similar proportions of New Zealand Year 5 boys and girls liked learning science and were conﬁ dent in 
science. This also held across both Māori and Pākehā/European girls and boys. 
Teachers and teaching science
Blömeke, Olsen, & Suhl (2016), in their recent research paper using TIMSS 2010/11 results, found that teacher 
qualiﬁ cations and measures of instructional quality were both signiﬁ cantly related to student outcomes. 
More New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers with postgraduate and higher degrees in 2014/15, compared 
to 2010/11. Only 15 percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers who had also specialised in science. 
Internationally, more students (38% on average) had teachers with a specialisation in science. Compared 
to mathematics, far fewer New Zealand Year 5 teachers had professional development in science. About 
60 percent of students were taught by teachers who hadn’t had any professional development in science 
teaching in the last 2 years. Compared with their international colleagues, on average, fewer New Zealand 
teachers engaged in professional development in science teaching. 
New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about how their teacher engaged with them in their science 
classes, but not as positive as on average internationally.
New Zealand Year 5 students spent just over an hour on science topics per week, which was almost half as 
much as the international average. Science was also more likely to be taught as an integrated subject in New 
Zealand. As well as spending less time teaching science than many other countries, New Zealand teachers 
used many of the science learning techniques less often, but had students look up ideas and information 
more often.
In contrast to mathematics lessons, New Zealand Year 5 students were more likely to work in mixed ability 
groups in their science lessons than work in same ability groups. There was increase in the emphasis on 
science experiment or investigation for Year 5 students in 2014/15, compared to 2010/11. New Zealand had 
the highest proportion of Year 5 students with computers available for use during science lessons, compared 
to all other countries. 
On average internationally, many more teachers placed major emphasis on monitoring students’ progress 
than in New Zealand.
School climate for learning
A positive school climate has been shown to be associated with academic achievement, as well as the 
behavioural and social-emotional wellbeing of students (see, for example, Voight, Austin, & Hanson, 2013). 
The TIMSS results for New Zealand conﬁ rm this ﬁ nding when the climate is examined from the point of view 
of students, teachers, principals and parents. 
Nine out of ten New Zealand Year 5 students were positive about their schools, teachers and classmates, and 
two thirds had a high sense of school belonging. Students who had a sense of belonging had higher science 
achievement, on average, than those with little sense of belonging at school.
There were more students in this latest cycle, compared with 2010/11, agreeing that they felt safe at school, 
liked being in school, and felt like they belonged at their school. However, New Zealand Year 5 students 
experienced bullying behaviours at school more frequently than most of the other participating countries. 
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
161
TIMSS 2014/15 | SCIENCE | YEAR 5
TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
Back to Contents
»

Compared to 2010/11, fewer students experienced the individual bullying behaviours: I was made fun of or 
called names; something was stolen from me; and I was hit or hurt (e.g. shoving, hitting, kicking) by other 
student(s). For all other behaviours, the proportion did not change signiﬁ cantly between the two cycles. 
Students who experienced bullying behaviours a few times a year or less often performed signiﬁ cantly better 
in science, on average, than those who experienced it once a month or more frequently. Bullying behaviours 
had the same impact on science achievement for Year 5 students across diff erent gender, ethnic groupings 
and socio-economic backgrounds.
So what did the teachers think about the climate for learning? The majority of New Zealand schools (79% 
of Year 5 students) had high or very high emphasis on academic success, according to teachers. Higher 
school emphasis on academic success was associated with higher science scores, both in New Zealand and 
internationally.
Half of the Year 5 students were taught by teachers who were very satisﬁ ed with their job; 97 percent of 
teachers were proud of the work they did. Seventy-one percent of New Zealand Year 5 students had teachers 
who felt their school was very safe and orderly.
Compared with many other countries, New Zealand teachers perceived more challenges in their teaching. 
They wanted to have more time to assist individual students, and preferred to have less administrative tasks. 
Three quarters of Year 5 students studied in classes where teachers felt that student needs were somewhat 
limiting their science teaching. These students had lower achievement than those who studied in classes 
where teachers felt that student needs did not limit teaching at all.
The principals were very positive about their teachers, with more than 80 percent of Year 5 students having 
principals who rated teachers’ role in the school’s academic success as very high or high. Principals were less 
positive about parents’ support and involvement than about their teachers and students. The principals and 
teachers had similar views on most aspects of the schools’ academic success, the only diff erence of opinion 
was observed on the students' respect for classmates who excel in school, about which the principals were 
more positive than the teachers.
Compared to other countries, New Zealand principals reported fewer problems of school discipline and 
safety. Over two-thirds of Year 5 students in New Zealand (71%) attended schools where the principal 
reported hardly any problems with school discipline and safety. Being in a school with less of a problem 
was associated with higher achievement in science, on average. There has been no change in the level of 
discipline and safety in New Zealand schools since TIMSS 2010/11. This ﬁ nding appears to contrast with 
student reports of bullying behaviours; New Zealand was one of the countries with the most students 
regularly experiencing bullying behaviours.
In general, parents had very positive perceptions of their child’s school. Students whose parents were less 
than satisﬁ ed with their child’s school had lower science achievement than those who were more satisﬁ ed. 
Nearly all parents (97%) agreed that their child’s school provided a safe environment for learning. A higher 
percentage of parents felt that schools were doing a good job in helping the students to become better in 
mathematics or reading than in science. Compared to 2010/11, in 2014/15 more parents agreed that their 
child’s school does a good job in helping him/her become better in science.
Final comment
As well as providing us with a snapshot of student achievement in science in middle primary and lower 
secondary schooling, TIMSS also provides us with valuable information about how the New Zealand 
education system changes — or does not — over time, and in an international context. This allows education 
stakeholders at all levels of the education system to reﬂ ect on the diff erent aspects examined in TIMSS, as 
part of a review of their policies and practices.

164
Sampling
Schools were sampled for Year 5 and Year 9 
separately but with the intent to minimise the 
overlap and therefore to minimise the burden 
on individual schools. They were sampled from 
pre-deﬁ ned groups. These pre-deﬁ ned groups, 
or explicit strata, were based on decile, level of 
urbanisation, and for Year 9 only, school gender. 
This methodology meant that the schools selected 
better represented the population of schools in 
New Zealand. Within each school, at the Year 5 
level, classes were sampled with equal probability, 
and all Year 5 students within each sampled class 
were selected. At the Year 9 level, 2 classes were 
sampled and all Year 9 students within each 
sampled class were selected.
Exclusions
Each country was permitted to exclude some 
students for whom the assessment was not 
appropriate or was diffi  cult to administer. Countries 
were required to keep the amount of excluded 
students as small as possible, with a guideline 
of ﬁ ve percent of the ‘target’ population as the 
maximum. Any countries that exceeded this 
value are indicated in the international exhibits. 
The target population in New Zealand for middle 
primary students was Year 5 students and for lower 
secondary was Year 9 students.
Deﬁ nitions and 
technical notes
School-level exclusions in New Zealand consisted of 
very small schools (less than four Year 5 students 
or less than four Year 9 students), special education 
schools, the Correspondence School, and schools 
that provide more than 50% of their instruction in 
te reo Māori. Within-school exclusions consisted of 
special education classes, special needs students, 
students with insuffi  cient instruction in English, and 
units within schools that provide more than 50% of 
their instruction in te reo Māori.
The New Zealand exclusion rate was 4.8% for Year 
5 and 3.1% for Year 9. 
Mean, medians, modes and 
averages
There are three measures of central tendancy, 
the mean, median, and mode. The mean of a set 
of scores is the sum of the scores divided by the 
number of scores, and is also sometimes referred 
to as ‘the average’, particularly in the international 
reports. Note that for TIMSS, as with other large-
scale studies, the means for a country are adjusted 
slightly (in technical terms ‘weighted’) to reﬂ ect the 
total population of Year 5 students, rather than just 
the sample. The mode is the value that appears the 
most out of all values. The median is the middle 
value when all the values are put in order.
This section gives a brief overview of the 
technical details and deﬁ nitions applicable 
to this report. For a comprehensive 
description of the technical details 
pertaining to TIMSS see Methods and 
Procedures in TIMSS 2015 (Martin, Mullis, 
& Hooper, (Eds.), 2016).

TRENDS OVER 20 YEARS IN TIMSS
165
Scale score points
The design of TIMSS allows for a large number of 
questions to be used in science and mathematics; 
each student answers only a portion of these 
questions. TIMSS employs techniques to enable 
population estimates of achievement to be 
produced for each country, even though a sample 
of students responded to diff ering selections of 
questions. These techniques result in scaled scores 
that are on a scale with a mean of 500 and a 
standard deviation of 100.
TIMSS scale centrepoint
In order to make comparisons, student 
achievement scores generated in each cycle 
are placed on the same scale. The scale was 
established during the second cycle of TIMSS to 
have a mean of 500 and a standard deviation of 
100, based on the mean of country means from 
1994/95. Equating is possible because a proportion 
of questions are the same in each assessment 
as for the two previous cycles. A score of 500 in 
2014/15 is the same as a score of 500 in all previous 
cycles.
In earlier cycles of TIMSS, an international mean 
was reported. However as the number of countries 
participating changed, this mean shifted so that 
it was diffi  cult to make comparisons across years. 
Starting from TIMSS 2010/11, only the TIMSS scale 
centrepoint of 500 is reported. This is the same 
as the TIMSS scale average reported in previous 
cycles, but renamed to avoid confusion with a 
calculated mean of country means. 
Minimum group size for 
reporting achievement data
In this report, student achievement data is not 
reported where the group size is less than 50 
students or less than 10 schools. 
Percentile
The percentages of students performing below or 
above particular points on the scale can be used 
to describe the range of achievement. The lowest 
outer limit of achievement reported in ranges is 
the 5th percentile – the score at which only ﬁ ve 
percent of students achieved a lower score, and 
95% of students achieved a higher score. The 
highest outer limit is the 95th percentile – the score 
at which only ﬁ ve percent of students achieved a 
higher score, and 95% of students achieved a lower 
score. Therefore 90% of the Year 5 student scores 
lie between the 5th and 95th percentiles.
The range of achievement
In this report, the range of achievement is the 
diff erence between the low percentile score 
(the score at which only ﬁ ve percent of students 
achieved a lower score) and the high percentile 
score (the score at which only ﬁ ve percent of 
students achieved a higher score).
Inter-quartile range
The inter-quartile range is more stable than the 
full range and is the diff erence between the 
25th percentile score (the score at which 25% 
of students achieved a lower score) and 75th 
percentile score (the score at which 25% of 
students achieved a higher score).
Benchmarks
In order to describe more fully what achievement 
on the science scale means, the TIMSS international 
researchers have developed benchmarks. These 
benchmarks link student performance on the 
TIMSS science scale to performance on science 
questions and describe what students can typically 
do at set points on the science achievement 
scale. The international science benchmarks are 
four points on the science scale; the advanced 
benchmark (625), the high benchmark (550), 
the intermediate benchmark (475), and the low 
benchmark (400). The performance of students 
reaching each benchmark is described in relation to 
the types of questions they answered correctly.
Content domains
These are the content areas of science, known 
as domains, covered by the TIMSS assessment 
framework. The content of science at the middle 
primary level is made up of three dimensions or 
domains; life science, physical science, and Earth 
science. (see chapter 3 for details).
Cognitive domains
The cognitive domains are the thinking processes 
and behaviours expected as students engage with 
the content ranging from simple knowledge recall 
to higher-order thinking skills, and include knowing, 
applying, and reasoning dimensions (see chapter 3 
for details).
www.educationcounts.govt.nz/goto/timss
Back to Contents
»

166
Test-curriculum matching 
analysis
In order to investigate the extent to which the 
TIMSS assessment is relevant to each country’s 
curriculum, TIMSS conducts a Test-Curriculum 
Matching Analysis (TCMA). The TCMA is also 
used to investigate the impact of selecting only 
appropriate questions on a country’s performance. 
(for details see the TIMSS International Database 
guide) 
Created scales for 
contextual variables
Item response theory was applied to student 
responses to questions so that clustering was 
taken into account. For example if nearly every 
student gave a highly positive response to one item 
then it did not overly contribute to the sum. Each 
respondent was then given a score which was put 
on a scale. Cut points on that scale were deﬁ ned 
and descriptions provided that detailed the kind of 
responses given in the original questions.
Ethnicity classification in 
TIMSS 2014/15
In previous cycles of TIMSS, New Zealand 
classiﬁ ed each student to one ethnic grouping 
using only prioritisation. In this cycle, any student 
who identiﬁ ed with more than one grouping was 
assigned to each of those groupings, in line with 
the Ethnicity New Zealand Standard Classiﬁ cation 
(Statistics New Zealand, 2005). TIMSS 2014/15 
classiﬁ ed the following ethnic groupings based on 
student responses: Māori, Pasiﬁ ka, Asian, Pākehā/
European, and Other. If a student ticked both Māori 
and Samoan, they would be classiﬁ ed as both 
Māori and Pasiﬁ ka. Using this methodology, ethnic 
groupings cannot be compared with each other 
as 22% of students are in more than one grouping 
(some are in as many as four groupings).
PIRLS
Stands for the Progress in International Reading 
Literacy Study and is being conducted under the 
auspices of the IEA (International Association 
for the Evaluation of Educational Achievement); 
managed internationally by Boston College; and 
managed within New Zealand by the Ministry of 
Education. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pirls
PISA 
Programme for International Student Assessment 
(PISA) is a triennial international survey which 
aims to evaluate education systems worldwide 
by testing the skills and knowledge of 15-year-old 
students. For more information visit: http://www.
educationcounts.govt.nz/topics/research/pisa
School decile
School deciles indicate the extent the school 
draws their students from low socio-economic 
communities. It is calculated every 5 years following 
the Census of Population and Dwellings, and based 
on 5 socio-economic indicators for a community: 
 »
Percentage of households with income in the 
lowest 20% nationally.
 »
Percentage of employed parents in the lowest 
skill level occupational groups.
 »
Household crowding.

made are tested for statistical signiﬁ cance using 
the t statistic, with the probability of making an 
incorrect inference set at ﬁ ve percent. To compare 
the means of two groups of students, the formula 
to generate the test statistics computed in this 
report is:

standard error of the diff erence is calculated as the 
square root of the sum of the squared standard 
errors of each mean:

used in these tests, which may account for some 
results appearing to be inconsistent.

where the sei are the standard errors of all the

Standard error
Because of the technical nature of TIMSS, the 
calculation of statistics such as means and 
proportions has some uncertainty due to (i) 
generalising from the sample to the total Year 5 
school population, and (ii) inferring each student’s 
proﬁ ciency from their performance on a subset 
of questions. The standard errors provide a 
measure of this uncertainty. In general, we can be 
95% conﬁ dent that the true population value lies 
within an interval of 1.96 standard errors either 
side of the given statistic. This conﬁ dence interval 
is represented in graphs by the lines extending in 
either direction from the points.
In this cycle of TIMSS, a new method for calculating 
the standard errors has been used. Therefore, the 
standard errors for previous cycles have been 
recalculated using this new method and will be 
diff erent from those reported earlier. This new 
methodology takes advantage of advances in 
computing and uses more information about 
variations in computations.
Statistically significant
In order to determine whether a diff erence 
between two means is actual, it is usual to 
undertake tests of signiﬁ cance. These tests take 
into account the means and the error associated 
with them. If a result is reported as not being 
statistically signiﬁ cant then, although the means 
might be slightly diff erent, we do not have 
suffi  cient evidence to infer that they are diff erent. 
All tests of statistical signiﬁ cance referred to in this 
report are at the 95% conﬁ dence level.
Weighting
Due to the use of sampling, weights need to be 
applied when analysing the TIMSS data. Weighting 
ensures that any information presented more 
closely reﬂ ects the total population of Year 5 
students, rather than just the sample. The TIMSS 
weighting takes into account school, class, and 
student level information, and the overall sampling 
weight is a product of the school, class, and 
student weights.
